I answer, that I confess it is making them the supreme rule of the thing that is to be tried, and that is whether the man agrees with me in those doctrines which I esteem fundamental or of great importance, which I may justly insist upon as a thing to be tried in order to some purposes, and particularly for that purpose, viz. whether to admit the man to such a degree of religious communion with me, or in order to admit the man as my intimate companion: as a voluntary club may insist upon it, in order to admission into their club, or any[^004-note128] other voluntary society may insist on it, in order to admission into [their society. Now I may justly make my own words the supreme rule of judgment, whether another man agrees with me in sentiment, or words that I shall accept. The thing I want to know, is whether he understands the Scripture right, or understands it as I do: this I may justly desire to know on some occasions, and in order to know, I must have some rule; and certainly, for him to repeat the words of Scripture, and say he believes these, don't determine. Nor are the Scriptures themselves the rule of determination in this matter; they are not the rule whereby to judge, whether his understanding of the Scripture and mine agree. Now my judging whether his understanding of the Scripture and mine agree, is the very same thing as judging what his real sentiments are. Now who will deny that, in order to some purposes, I may, if I please, insist on knowing and being satisfied what a man's real sentiments are—as in choosing a wife, or a tutor for my children, etc., etc.—and in order to this, I may choose[^004-note129] my words, or choose what words I will accept? Refusing this is great tyranny indeed. My own words, or words that satisfy my judgment as a real signification of his sentiments to my judgment, are properly the supreme rule of judgment of what is my mind.

Here is a grand mistake in this affair: The thing to be judged here, is not what doctrine is truth, but what doctrine the man believes; what are his sentiments, and not what are true sentiments, in their true interpretation. Now the words of Scripture are the highest rule of truth, but are not at all the rule to determine what his sentiments are, as is most demonstrable by fact.

And then, besides, the words of Scripture themselves] are no rule of truth, but only in their right sense. The mere words, as made up of so many letters or syllables, are no rule at all, detached from some sense, any more than any sounds, or figures on paper whatsoever. If they be, then the Hebrew words of the Old Testament, pronounced by one that understands not a word of Hebrew, and the Greek words of the New, said by one that learnt to read it but understands not a word of it, is a sufficient [declaration. If he says he believes those doctrines, declared in those words, is a sufficient declaration to me that he believes all those doctrines: no, surely, I may know whether he understands those words, and not only whether he understands something by 'em, but whether he understands 'em right or not; and the words of the Scripture are not the rule to determine. And if I may insist on knowing whether he rightly understands the words, so I may whether he rightly understands the phrases; i.e. I may insist on knowing whether he understands 'em right in my judgment, according as I have judged for myself of the reasons to determine both the sense of words and phrases; and my own judgment, or what satisfies my judgment, is the proper rule, and the supreme rule, of determination of this matter.

Many of these gentlemen hold that reason is the highest rule, and the rule by which we must judge of Scripture itself. And therefore, what need men say they believe the Scripture? Why is it not sufficient to say they believe the dictates of reason in general?

Making my judgment or my own words a test of judging, whether others agree with me in understanding the Scripture, in what I think important for such purposes, is not to set up another rule to bind the consciences of men, or pretending to lay the least constraint on conscience.

'Tis not substituting other rules of faith in the room of the holy Scriptures, or setting another rule above the Scripture, or in addition to it.

Concerning its being DANGEROUS that the REAL TRUTH should be KNOWN in some instance, its being best to say little about it, and to conceal it from the common people, to disguise it, etc., lest a bad improvement should be made of it, because they cant comprehend it, etc., etc.

Concerning that which Tindal, and some others, says, that if a proposition is proposed to our belief that we can't comprehend, nothing is really proposed to belief, see "Miscellanies," no. 1340.[^004-note130]

Concerning MYSTERIES, see largely, "Miscellanies," no. 1340.[^004-note131]

'Tis a known thing that men understand the words and phrases of Scripture in innumerable, different, yea, very vastly different and utterly inconsistent senses. Therefore, nothing can be more plain, than that] a man's saying he assents to those words and phrases, can be NO TEST to try his opinions; or, in other words, no test by which one man can be determined what another man's opinions are, and therefore in no respect a trial of the ORTHODOXY or truth of his opinions. For it cannot be known whether his opinions are true, till it be first known what his opinions be. Words avail as a means to signify of men's minds by use, and no otherwise, whether they be the words of Scripture, or any other words. There being words that the Scriptures have made use [of, alters not the case in the least.

If a man is going a journey, and inquires, of a great number of paths, which is the right, and is told, 'tis that which leads south, it can determine him nothing at all if the word "south," as used in that place, has 32 different and inconsistent significations: one by the word means what another calls "north," another what he means by "northwest"; and so it is understood of all the 32 points of the compass.

There are two things to be tried, which men have a right to insist on being determined in, in order to various purposes: one is, what a man's opinions are, the other is whether those opinions are what I esteem true or not, in an important manner or degree wide from truth—neither of which the WORDS OF SCRIPTURE ARE THE TEST OF.

FUTURE PUNISHMENT OF THE UNGODLY

The certainty of their punishment, see note on Proverbs 26:10.[^004-note132]

The same disposition, and habit of mind, and manner of viewing things, that is indeed the main ground of the cavils of many of the modern free thinkers and modish writers against the extremity and eternity of hell torments, if given way to and relied upon, would cause 'em to be dissatisfied with almost anything that is very uncomfortable in a future punishment—so much as the enduring the pain that is occasioned] by some such thing as the digging out of an eye, or the thrusting a thorn under the nail of the finger for a whole year together, 365 days, day and night without any rest, or the least intermission or abatement. In short, it will be found that there will be no satisfying[^004-note133] this infidel humor, with anything that is very contrary to men's inclinations. Anything that they are very averse to bear, they would be averse to believe. There are innumerable calamities that come to pass in this world, through the permission and ordination of divine providence, which, were it not that they are what we see with our eyes and are universally known and incontestible facts, this caviling, unbelieving spirit would strongly object to; and if they were only proposed in the theory as matters of faith, would be opposed as exceeding inconsistent with the moral perfections of God, and the opinions of such as asserted them cried out of as innumerable ways contrary to God's wisdom, his justice, goodness, mercy, etc.: such as innumerable calamities that have happened to poor, innocent children through the merciless cruelty of barbarous enemies, their being gradually roasted at the fire by Indians, shrieking and crying for their fathers and mothers; the extreme pains they sometimes are tormented to death with by some terrible diseases which they suffer; the calamities that have many times been brought on whole cities while besieged, and when taken by merciless soldiers, destroying all, men, women and children without any pity; the extreme miseries which have been suffered by millions of all ages, sexes and conditions of innocent persons, in times of persecution, when there has been no refuge to be found on earth; yea, those things that come to pass universally, which all mankind are the subjects of in temporal death, which is so dreadful to nature, and which the human nature, which God has made, is so extreme[ly reluctant to. There is no trust at all to such notions and views, such seemings, as are the main ground of these men's objecting against the torments of hell as revealed in the Scripture. The main thing is, it is terrible, and so seems shocking, to the[^004-note134] inward apprehension of their minds, and this they call a being shocking to common sense, when it is so indeed no otherwise than as it is very opposite to common inclinations.[^004-note135]

To take particular notice, as an argument of the extremity of the future misery of the wicked, of that place, Psalms 73:19, "How are they brought into desolation, as in a moment! they are utterly consumed with terrors," especially that last clause.

[^004-note4]: The passage, from a previous section of the "Controversies," reads:After I have proved that true grace differs not only in degree but nature and kind from common grace, and that it is infused and men are made saints in an instant, then by way of COROLLARY observe that that virtue which has been obtained only by contracted habits, by custom, is not saving virtue. Here acknowledge a certain virtue may be obtained this way. Observe particularly after what manner. But then show wherein this differs from saving virtue. Also here allow that 'tis possible that God infuses grace at some instant into the minds of such persons as are striving to obtain it in the other way, and they may not observe it, and may not know that it is not obtained by gradual contraction. But if a man has indeed sought it only in that way, and with as much dependence on himself and with as much neglect of God in his endeavors and prayers, as such a doctrine naturally leads to, 'tis not very likely that he should obtain saving grace by the efficacious mighty power of God. Observe in what way it is most likely that God should bestow this gift, {viz.} in a way of earnest seeking of it by prayer to him together with the use of the means of grace, with reflection on one's own sinfulness and in a way of being more and more convinced of sinfulness and total corruption, and need of the divine power to restore the heart, to infuse goodness, and in becoming more and more sensible of his own impotence and helplessness and inability to obtain goodness by his own strength. And if he has obtained no other virtue than what seems to have been wholly in that gradual way, to have been by little and little advanced in that insensible way that might be expected from use and custom in the exercise of his own strength, he has reason to think, however bright his attainments may seem to be, that he has no saving virtue. Observe how great a part of the gospel is denied by them who deny such efficacious grace. They deny that wherein actual salvation and the application of redemption mainly consists, and how little likely such are to be succeeded in their endeavors after actual salvation. JE then deletes: "After having shown that there is NO TRUE VIRTUE in natural men, then observe how many ways virtue is resembled in them and sure sic of the ways of working of natural CONSCIENCE. Show the great difference between the dictates of conscience and divine taste. Here observe what that is which is commonly called MORAL TASTE and supposed to be natural to all men."

[^004-note7]: Turnbull, *Christian Philosophy*, p. 470. On p. 472, Turnbull states that theevidence which Christ gave for the truth of his doctrine concerning the Governor and government of the world, must be a full and proper evidence of its truth; or it must be said, either that he did not give sufficient samples of his benevolence to mankind, his regard to truth, honesty, and sincerity, which was never asserted, such an uninterrupted series of generosity, benevolence, and sincerity was his life: or that the many works he wrought of the kinds above mentioned, were not samples of a very large insight into, and power in nature; which will be to affirm that samples of power to see into men's minds, and foretell their future actions; power to change men's minds; power to deliver from evils of all sorts, corporeal and mental; power to confer gifts of all sorts, bodily and spiritual; power to raise the dead; power to transfer to others this same extensive power he himself was possessed of, did not shew a very large and extensive knowledge of nature, and power in nature. William Warburton, *Principles of Natural and Revealed Religion*, *Occasionally Opened & Explained; in a course of sermons* (2 vols. London, 1753), *1*, 234-35. The passage JE refers to is in Sermon VI, "The Office and Operation of the Holy Spirit." The pages in question begin a section considering the Holy Spirit "as the COMFORTER, who rectifies and supports the will":And here again his divine power manifests itself in as miraculous an operation. Sacred antiquity is very large and full in its accounts of the sudden and entire change the Holy Spirit made in the dispositions and manners of those it inlightened; instantaneously effacing all their evil habits, and familiarizing their practice to the performances of every good and virtuous action. To this illustrious and triumphant Truth, the very enemies and persecutors of our holy faith have been forced to subscribe: not only in the serious accounts which some of them give of the virtue and innocence of PRIMITIVE CHRISTIANITY; but even in the ironical declamations of others concerning the boasted virtue of water-baptism.

[^004-note8]: Turnbull, *Christian Philosophy*, p. 282:If we reflect, in the first place, that, though some few may, through the good influence of virtuous example, and of a wise and happy education, be said to be *sanctified from the womb*, so liberal, so generous, so virtuous, so truly noble is their cast of mind; yet generally speaking, men are so corrupt, the whole world always hath, and still *lieth in such wickedness*, that with respect to the far greater part of mankind the study of virtue is beginning to reform, and is a severe struggle against bad habits early contracted, and deeply rooted. It is therefore putting off an old inveterate corrupt nature, and putting on a new form and temper: it is moulding ourselves anew: it is being *born again*, and becoming as *children*, to be formed into a right shape; becoming docile, tractable, and pliable, as little children, in order to be instructed in, and formed to the temper which becomes rational creatures. . .

[^004-note12]: Joseph Butler, *The analogy of religion*,*natural and revealed, to the constitution and course of nature*(London, 1736), esp. pp. 144-48, in which he considers Christianity as "a Republication of natural Religion." Turnbull, *Christian Philosophy*, pp. 183-84:That miraculous powers should be exerted at such times, upon such occasions, in such degrees and manners, and with regard to such persons rather than others; that the affairs of the world, being permitted to go on in their natural course so far, should just at such a point have a new direction given them by miraculous interpositions; that these interpositions should be exactly in such degrees and respects only; all this may have been by general laws. These laws are indeed unknown to us: but no more unknown than several other laws from whence certain effects proceed are, though it is taken for granted, they are as much reducible to general ones as gravitation.. .. This reasoning from analogy is certainly very just. But it is not from analogy merely, that we have reason to conclude, that the whole of the divine government must be by general laws; and consequently, that even miraculous interpositions must be all along carried on by general laws, tho' unknown to us.. .. It cannot therefore be a scheme which frequently requires interpositions, at certain points, till then never thought of by the ruler of the world, and maker of all things.. .. But if miraculous interpositions cannot mean such extemporary, unpremeditated or casual interpositions, *pro re nata*; 'tis plain, miraculous interpositions can be in no other sense miraculous to us, than any other effects are really such, whose laws are unknown to us, tho' we do not call all such miraculous. The larger section of Turnbull's discourse to which JE is referring, as described in the table of contents, is section II, "The Scripture doctrine concerning providence more fully explained, etc.," proposition III (pp. 173-92), "The divine, infinitely wise, just, faithful, good providence governs the whole universe by general laws; nor is what is said in Scripture of special, miraculous interpositions of providence inconsistent with such government."

IV, "Of a State of Probation, as intended for moral Discipline and Improvement," pp. 79-103, and "Of the Nature of Virtue," pp. 309-20; Turnbull, *Principles of Moral Philosophy*: *An Enquiry Into the wise and good Government of the Moral World* (London, 1740), Pt. I, ch. 5, "Laws relative to interest, or private and publick good" (see following note); and *Christian Philosophy*, Sec. III, Prop. II (pp. 258-77): "According to revelation, we are made and placed in our present state chiefly to endeavour to attain to the love of the pleasures arising from rational, virtuous exercise; and to the contempt of mere sensual pleasure, in comparison of them: And this reason itself plainly proves to be the chief end of our being, from the very nature of our frame, and from our present situation, which are admirably well adapted one to another." According to the description in the table of contents, on p. 263 Turnbull argues that "The natural happiness of a being must be similar to, of a kind with, and result from its frame and composition. We are therefore made for rational exercises, and virtuous happiness"; and on p. 269, that "acting agreeably to reason is always our best interest, or greatest happiness. .. This is the language of reason, as well as of revelation, That virtue by its sufferings shall be fitted for a peculiarly glorious share of after-happiness."

[^004-note43]: Turnbull, *Christian Philosophy*, pp. 289-91:And therefore it is, that virtue is justly said to be its own reward, or in other words, that the glory prepared for the virtuous, in a future state, is called grace, or virtue made perfect, and placed in circumstances for exercises adequate to its perfection.. .. And those who assert, that there is no obligation to virtue independently of the consideration of future rewards and punishments, do absurdly assert (in whatever sense they take obligation) that there is a happiness hereafter for the virtuous, not of the virtuous or rational kind, which makes the only good reason for the study of virtue here: or, in other words, that it is wise and prudent to be virtuous here, merely because in another life the virtuous may be as unvirtuous as they please; because they shall then be released from their obligations to troublesome, virtuous exercises, and shall have their belly full of other delights far superior to all that virtue can by its noblest exercises afford to a rational mind.. .. There is indeed none, nor can there, in the nature of things, be any argument to prove a future state, which does not suppose rational exercises to be the best, the noblest, and pleasantest exercises of reasonable beings, and which for that reason does not suppose, that, if there be a state of rewards for virtue, it must be a state in which virtue shall reap happiness, proportioned to its perfection from exercises and objects suited to it.

[^004-note44]: JE wrote "to my book" in shorthand, possibly to keep secret its existence from any students of his who may have been reading his manuscripts. The reference may be to a now-missing draft of this treatise, or to a former one that JE retained, or to a set of notes. It was JE's habit, when writing a lengthy work, to construct a temporary series of small books in which he wrote notes, reminders, and references (see, for example, "Sacrament Bk.1" and "Religious Affections, Bk. 7"). He would then use these, along with entries from his regular notebooks, to write a full draft in bound volumes (as in the two quarto volumes in which he wrote the draft of *Misrepresentations Corrected*).

[^004-note95]: Andrew Michael Ramsay, *The Philosophical Principles of Natural and Revealed Religion, unfolded in a geometrical order* (Glasgow, 1748/49), pp. 22-23:The last branch of the postulate is, that "we ought never to deny because we cannot conceive." If this were not so, then a man born blind would reason right when he forms this syllogism, "We know the figure of bodies only by handling them, but it is impossible to handle them at a great distance; therefore it is impossible to know the figure of far distinct bodies." To undeceive the blind man. .. We may prove to him that this is so from the concurrent testimony of all who surround him; but we can never make him perceive how this is so.. .. It is therefore a fundamental maxim in all true philosophy, that "many things may be incomprehensible and yet demonstrable, that tho' seeing clearly be a sufficient reason for affirming; yet not seeing at all can never be a reason of denying."

[^004-note99]: Ramsay, *Philosophical Principles of Natural and Revealed Religion*, p. 74: "Hence the first mystery of natural religion is that of the divine immensity, or unextensive existence: we see that it must be, but not how it is; this attribute is incomprehensible but not impossible. It is unconceivable, but it is demonstrable. Now when we have demonstrated that a thing must be, we ought not to deny it purely and only, because we cannot conceive it."

[^004-note100]: John McLaurin, *Sermons and Essays* (Glasgow, 1755), "An Essay on Christian Piety," pp. 172 ff. In his introduction (pp. 172-73), McLaurin states that he intends to take "a short comprehensive view of the Christian doctrines, as is requisite for explaining the chief parts and characters of Christian piety. The chief peculiar docrtrines of the gospel relate to the divine promises of salvation and happiness; the principal causes of that salvation, particularly the mercy and power of God in the mediation of his Son, and the effectual grace of his Spirit; the divine nature and glory of both: the means of that salvation on our part, namely the means of an interest in the divine promises, and the means of obtaining the accomplishment of them; and particularly the constant improvement that ought to be made of all the grounds of our hope and joy as motives to love and obedience." P. 189, the terminus cited by JE, takes the argument up through "habitual knowledge of Christ's mediation." The other work is Christoph Matthaeus Pfaff, *Institutiones theologiæ dogmaticæ et moralis* (Tubingen, 1720), and attests to the Pietiest influence on JE.

[^004-note101]: Locke, *Essay* (7th ed.), vol. 1, Bk. II, ch. 11, sec. 2, p. 117:How much the imperfection of accurately discriminating *Ideas* one from another. .. is of that consequence to its other Knowledge, that so far as this faculty is in it self dull, or not rightly made use of, for the distinguishing one thing from another; so far our Notions are confused, and our Reason and Judgment disturbed or misled. If in having our *Ideas* in the Memory ready at hand, consists quickness of parts; in this of having them unconfused, and being able nicely to distinguish one thing from another, where there is but the least difference, consists, in a great measure, the exactness of Judgment, and clearness of Reason, which is to be observed in one Man above Another.. .. *Judgment*, on the contrary, lies quite on the other side, in separating carefully, one from another, *Ideas*, wherein can be found the least difference, thereby to avoid being misled by Similitude, and by affinity to take one thing for another.

[^004-note102]: Turnbull, *Principles of Moral Philosophy*, p. 94: "Judgment .. . is rightly said to lie in nicely distinguishing the disagreements and variances or differences of ideas; those especially which lie more remote from common observation, and are not generally adverted to.. .. The man of judgment or discretion (for so discretion properly signifies) may be defined to be one who has a particular aptitude to discry differences of all kinds between objects, even the most hidden and remote from vulgar eyes."

## Part V Controversies 5 (ed.)

Jonathan Edwards

### PREDESTINATION

This section contains a great number of relocated passages, as directed by JE through cue signs and prompts, and made more complicated by JE Jr.'s markings. An attempt has been made to follow JE's intentions, but the final order of some of the passages must remain conjectural.

Mr. Grove, in his discourse on *Wisdom the First Spring of Action in the Deity*,Henry Grove, *Wisdom the First Spring of Action in the Deity* (London, 1734), p. 2:It is not a thing indifferent, whether the *end* propos'd by God in creating intelligent beings, shall be *the display of his sovereign uncontroulable power in their everlasting misery, without the least thing done by them to deserve it; raising their expectations by express and solemn promises, and then disappointing them* .. .. The sun in the heavens is not more visible by its own light, than the truth of this *maxim*, that *whoever knowingly and wilfully hurts another, without cause, is guilty of injustice*; and the greater the *hurt*, the greater the *injustice*. objects thus against the doctrine of REPROBATION, p. 2: "It is not a thing indifferent, whether the end proposed by God in creating intelligent beings, shall be the display of his sovereign, uncontrollable power in their everlasting misery, without the least thing done by them to deserve it; raising their expectations by express and solemn promises, and then disappointing them .. .. The sun in the heavens is not more visible by its own light, than the truth of this maxim, that *whoever knowingly and willfully hurts another without cause, is guilty of injustice*; and the greater the hurt, the greater the injustice."

That place, Matthew 20:21-23, "Grant that these my two sons may sit, one on thy right hand, and the other on thy left, in thy kingdom.. .. It shall be given to them for whom it is prepared of my Father," affords an invincible argument for particular, personal predestination.

'Tis an evidence that the Apostle, in the 9th of Romans, has not respect only to an election and dereliction of nations or public societies, because one instance that he produces to illustrate and confirm what he says, is the dereliction of a particular person, even Pharaoh, Romans 9:17. So 'tis an instance of God's mercy to a particular person, even Moses, when he says to Moses, "I will have mercy on whom I will have mercy, and will have compassion," etc. Romans 9:15.

The words cited were used by God on occasion of, and with relation to, his mercy to a particular person, even Moses; and the language in that verse, and the next, is suited to particular persons, as v. 16, and v. 18, and vv. 22-23. And the Apostle shows plainly, vv. 27, 29, that 'tis not an election of nations or public societies, but a distinction of some particular persons from others of the same society; as it was a distinction of particular persons in preserving some, when others were destroyed by Nebuchadnezzar's armies, and in returning some from captivity and leaving others. This was a showing mercy to one public society in distinction from another.

So in ch. 10:4-5, where the Apostle plainly continues to speak of the same election, it was not by a national election, or election of any public society, that God distinguished the seven thousand that he had reserved, that had not bowed the knee to the image of Baal 1 Kings 19:18, Romans 11:4.

To consider particularly how God is said to be willing that all men should be saved, etc. How he has the same love, all that goodness, that is requisite in order to this, so that those same qualifications, if imparted only in a small communication and image of 'em in one that is not omniscient, and to whom does not belong the sovereign disposal of all things, it will actually have such an exercise. As in the image of God's goodness in the saints, and when God becomes man, in the man Christ Jesus. WILLINGNESS, INVITATIONS, etc.

GOD'S DECREEING SIN. "It ought to be observed, that as God foreknows the wickedness of which free agents will be guilty, so his decree with respect to their wickedness, is no other than a decree to permit them to act freely; and surely there is not the least violation of their freedom in this. This is in effect God's decreeing that they shall commit this or that wickedness, on supposition of God's foreknowing that they will certainly be guilty of it, if suffered to follow their own inclinations." Greene, *Friendly Conferences on Taylor's "Key,"* pp. 88-89.Pub. 1756; no further information found on this title.

N.B. It appears probable, by Greene's *Friendly Conferences*, a pamphlet Mr. BurrRev. Aaron Burr. lent me, published in 1756, that Mr. Locke's explanations of the texts in Paul's Epistles, which speak of election and predestination, are now pretty generally embraced by the Arminians, both of the Church of England and dissenters.

"John 6:37, 39, 'All that the Father hath given me shall come to me; .. . And this is the Father's will which sent me, that of all which he hath given me I should lose nothing, but should raise it up again at the last day.' What is this, the being given to Christ to be raised up again, and, as v. 4, to everlasting life, but the election of particular persons to salvation? And since it is the Father's will that, of all that he has given to Christ, he should lose nothing, this election must be so absolute as to insure their salvation." Greene's *Friendly Conferences*.

To improve that argument, that election is said not to be of WORKS. Romans 9:11, "that the purpose of God according to election might stand, *not of works*, but of him that calleth"; v. 11, "neither of them *having done either good or evil*"; and Romans 11:5-6, "Even so at this present time also there is a remnant according to the *election of grace*. And if by grace, then it is no more *of works*: otherwise grace is no more grace. But if it be of works, then it is no more grace: otherwise work is no more work"; 2 Timothy 1:9, "who hath saved us, and called us with an holy calling, *not according to our works*, but according to his own purpose and grace, which was given us in Christ Jesus before the world began."

To observe particularly how invincible a proof of the Calvinist doctrine of election that place is, in Romans 11:5, "Even so, then at this present time also there is a remnant according to the election of grace." Dr. Doddridge observes upon it, that some explain this of having chosen grace, i.e. the gospel.Doddridge, *Family Expositor*. But that term is very unnatural, and neither suits the phrase, nor the connection with the former clause or with the next verse, in which the Apostle comments on his own words.

If God don't some way in his providence, and so in his predetermination, order what the VOLITIONS of men shall be, heJE Jr.'s insert. would be as dependent in governing the world as a skillful mariner is in governing his vessel, in passing over a turbulent, tempestuous ocean, where he meets constantly, and through the whole voyage, with things that agitate the ship, have great influence on the motions of his ship, that are very cross and grievous to him, that he is obliged to accommodate himself to in the best manner that he can: he meets with cross winds, violent tempests, strong currents and great opposition from enemies, none of which things he has the disposal of, but is forced to suffer; he only guides the ship, and by his skill turns that hither and thither, and steers it in such a manner as to avoid dangers as well as the case will allow.

ELECTIONJE Jr. draws a circle around this entry. of Collective Bodies. See papers pinned at the end of this book.Not extant.

To showMS: "shew in." particularly in what respect God has regard to conditions in his decree, as God has regard to a wise order and connection of things in a wise decree. Such is his wisdom in his decrees, and all acts and operations, that if it were not for wise connection that is regarded, many things would not be decreed. One part of the wise system of events would not have been decreed, unless the other had been decreed, etc.

Ut contra nos probent Rerum futurarum Existentiam sive Futuritionem non a divina Prædeterminatione ac Præscientia sed nonnisia libera summanæ voluntatis Electione pendere, ita ut Certitudo non sit antecedens et Causalis, sed subsequens & Eccentualis, et nihil aliud sit quam Notitia Rerum quare futuræ sunt, nec Necessitatem nec Causalitatem inferat; ad ca provocant scripturæ dicta ubide Præscientia conditionata sermo est Unde ita Burnetus, scriptura, inquit pluribus in locis, Præscientiam Conditionatam clare decet: indicavit Deus Davidi 2 Samuel 23:11-12. Saulim Keilam venturam, et Keilitos ipsum Regi tradituras esse. Utrumque vera futurum erat, sub ista Conditione, si ibi manerit; discedente vere Davide; neutiquam accidit. Hic erat Præscientia conditionata. sic secundum Effatum Christi Matthew 11:21-22. Tyrrii et Sydonii Sodomæi et Gomorræi pænitentiam acture suisient, si vidissent miracula, inquibusdam Galileæ Urbibus edita. Quandiquidem ergo hæc Præscientia tam certa esse potest, ut munquam erret, neque Providentiæ divinæ Counlia vona raddat; Quandoquidem etiam per hance Attributa divina vindicantur, et Libertas Voluntatis humanæ asseritur, omnes difficultates facillime hoc modo salvi videatur.

Responsio. Tenandum hic Intellectum divinum in se specatum, sive respecta ad Voluntatem, Radicem esse meræ Possibilitatis Rerum; dum deus intuendo semetipsum sive Perfectiones suas, adeoque cognoscendo seipsum, seit quicqui mullam Contradictionem involvit, sive sit Veritas absolute necessaria, sive contingens, sive libera. Sed hactenus Res nondum futurae sunt; sed nonnisi mare possibiles. Voluntas autem dei Radix Futuritionis est; ut propter divinæ Voluntatis Pradeterminationem, certa rerum series, et Rerum possibilium systema aliquod certo extitunum sit; sed tale quole indivino Intellectu, antecedenter ad decretum representatum suit; ut ea in illo libera maneant quætunc cen libera in divino Intellectu representata fuere, et contingentia quæ contingentia fuere. Quando vera divino voluntas Nexum diquem rerum ad actum producendam, adeoque futurum decrevit, divinus Intellectus illam rerum seriam non amplius ceu mere possibilem, sed etiam ceu futurem sibi representat. Concedum autem Adversaris ud saltem negare non poterunt, simplicem rerum ceu mere possibilium indivinio Intellectu Representationem antecedenter ad Decretum, Rebus nullam plane inferre Necessitatem, dum hactenus, dum hactenus, nullum ad divinam voluntatem respectu habito, ne quidem definitum sit, num res illæ ad Actum sint producendæ nec ne. Voluntas Autem tales res illas producere deceruit, quales in divino Intellectu representatæ fuere; ut nulla, respectu rei ipsius, per decretum mutetur Qualitas sive Affectio, nisi quod futura est. Unde nulla in Rebus earumque intrinseca Natura per decretum facta mutatione, condant oportent Adversarii, condem, juxta nostram de decretis doctrinam, intrinsecam omnium rerum menere Naturam, qualis antecendenter ante decretum, sive divinæ voluntatis Actum, in divino Intellectu representata suit. Cum autem sola Intellectuer divini Representatio, nullo ad ejus Voluntatem respectu habito, Rebus sive Rerum Essentiis, nullam inferat Necessitatem; Voluntatis ore actus, sive dicretum nihil plane in intrinseca rerum Natura, sive earum Essentiis muter. Sed eas tales sin at quales antea fuerent; sequitur nec Dei Voluntatem Absolutam Rebus liberis ac Contingentibus, inferre Necessitatem. Post quam autem divina Voluntas decrevit Rerum oliquam Seriem ae Nexam, antea ceu mere possibilem in divino Intellectu representatum, ad Actum producere, Intellectus divinus illud rerum systema, sve seriom illam non amplius ceu mere possibilem, sed ceu futuram sibi representat, eamque præcognoscit ac prævidet; Quæ Procognitio sive Prævisio non magis res illas absolute necessarioes facere potest ac id decretum facere demonstravimus, dum et hæc Prævisia in re decreta nihil plane muter. Unde et hic Adversarii debent fateri, Res ex decreto præcognitas propterea necessarias non fieri, nisi illarum Essentia jam antea feurint necessaria Adeoque deus omnes res tales futuras præcognoscit quales eas producere decrevit, quales sibi divinus Intellectus, jam antecedenter ad decretum ceu mere possibiles sibi representavit. Atqui Representatio illa, nullam Rerum Contingentium ac liberarum Necessitatem absolutam involvit. Ergo, nec earum ex decreto Præcognitio sive Prævisio.

Si dicunt Adversarii, Deo in Literis S. Præscientiam adscribi conditionatam a Creatinarum Actionibus liberis pendentem. Respondemus, Omnine Præscientiam divinam, respectu Creaturaru, sive objective consideratiam conditionatam esse — dum in toto rerum Nexu omnia sunt hypothetica ut si hoc fit; etiam illud fiat; ut tota series, ceu totodam Media, sive Conditions, sibi subordinata sit, ut hoc eveniat propter hoc; Adeoque tota rerum futurarum Præscientia conditionata est; dum Præscientia unius Rei semper presupponat alteram Rem. Nihilominus tamea certo futura sunt omnia, qua deus præicit, licet conditionata maneant. Non ergo Adversarii habent cur nos accusent, nos conditionatem Præscientiam negare.

Staturmus poor et nos, deum varias rerum series sibi representare, et quidem tales quæ in Statu meræ Possibilitatis permanent, nea unquam eventuræ sunt; Ut non solum sciet quid futurum sit, sed quid aliud etiam in alia rerum serie possible esset. So itague Deus Davidi indicavit Saluem Keilam venturum, et Keilotus ipsum Regi tradituros esse; Aut si Christus dicit Tyrios & Sydonias Pænitentiam Acturos suisse, so miracula vidissent in quibus dam Gililaæ urbibus edita, Nihil aliud indicatur, quam deum varias rerum Possibilium series, nunquam tamen eventuras, sibi representere; ut sciat quidnam per alium rerem Nexum futurum fuisset, si hæc vel illa Conditio, alterias Loco substitua fuisset. Concedimus itaque Præscientiam divinam ratione Objecti conditionatam esse; sed eas dam Conditiones, vel futuras vel non futuras, a divino Intellectu ac Voluntate pendere ascerimus; ut Deus aliam Rerum seriem, alias Conditiones, respectu rerum Contingentium ac liberarum ordinare ac eligere potuisset si voluisset.Johan Stapfer, *Institutiones Theologicae Polemicae*, 5? (Location of quote not found.)

Hæc veritas inconsussa manet nullum proprie dari decretorum Ordinem, sed omnia simul, uti unico Actu deus sibi repræsentavit, Pari Ratione etiam suisse decreta.

Non possumus non hic allegare illustris Leibnitii verba Hinc patet, inquit, Quæstionem inter SUPRALAPSARIOS et INFRALAPSARIOS ex parte, tum deinde hos inter et Evangelicos eo redire, ut bene concipiatur Ordo qui est in decretis divinis. Sed fortasse Disputatio hæc tota amputari repente posset, cunctis rite expensis statuendo, omnia dei decreta esse simul, non solum Tempore, de quo Nemo dubitat, sed etiam signo Rationis, vel Ordine Naturæ. Et certe Formula Concordiæ faventibus aliquot S. Augustini Textibus, et Electionem Salutis, et Media eo perducentia, unico eodemque decreto complexa est. Ut vero simultaneitas ista destinationum sive decretorum de quibus agitur, melius ostendatur, recurrendum est ad Methodum, qua plus semel jam usus sum, quæ huc redit, ut Deus antequam quidquam decerneret, inter alias seriesStapfer, 5, 186, reads: "alias rerum series." possibiles, consideravit eam quoque quam postea approbavit; In cujus Idea omnia futura representabantur: Quomodo sciliet peccaturi essent primi Parentes, et Posteritatem suam depravaturi; quo Pacto Christus humanum Genus redempturus esset; quomoStapfer reads: "quomodo." Pars, per varias Gratiæ Vias, ad Fidem, vivam finalem, ad Salutem esset perventura; et quomodo alli, vel adjuti vel non adjuti, iisdem aliisve Gratiæ donis, in Peccato perseveraturi et damnandi essent. Eadem deinde Methodus postulat, ut Deus censeatur non approbasse hanc seriem, nisi ubi totem sigillatim expendisset, atque adeo Nihil decretorie statuisse circa salvandos et damnandos, nisi prius omnibus eam ingredientibus ponderatis, & cum contentis aliarum serierum possibilium comparatis. Ita fit ut id quod decernit Deus totam simul seriem complectatur, cui duntaxat Existentiam Nutu suo impertitur ac proinde ad alios vel alio Modo salvandos necesse fuisset aliam omnino seriem generalem eligi; omnia enim in serie qualibet connexa intelliguntur. Itaque in hoc considerandi Modo, qui sapiente dignissimus est, cujus Actiones, quam fieri potest, maxime conspirant; nonnisi unum decretum dei totale Locum habet; nempe decretum creandi talem Mundum: quod aequaliter omnia decreta particularia complectitur, nullo inter illa ordine relicto. Quamvis enim ex alio Capite dici possit quemlibet actum particularem voluntatis antecedentis in Collectione totali contentum habere suum Pretium et ordinem proportionem boni in quod inclinat, hi tamen Actus Voluntatis antecedentis non sunt decreta, cum necdum sint infallibiles, finales, decisivi, quia successus eorum adhuc dependet, a Collectione Inclinationum totali, aliis Collectionibus, sive alteri cuicunque seriei prævalitura. Ita decretum nonnisi de tota fertur. Re ita sumta cessant omnes agitatæ vulgo difficultates circa ordinem decretorum, eæque tantum supersunt quas jam submovimus.

Cum decreta divina ex Reformatæ Ecclesiæ Mente sint ordinata, ut Media a fine nunquam sint sejungenda; hinc illorum sententiam rejicimus, qui statuunt, deum ante omnem Mediorum Considerationem nonnullos eligisse, alios vero reprobasse, nullo vel usus mediorum salutis, vel Peccati et finalis Resistentiæ Respectu habito. Neque toli Eccesliæ attribuendum id quod privatus aliquis doctor professus esse videtur.

Stapferus, *Theologiæ Polemicæ*, Tom. V, pp. 185, etc.Johan Stapfer, *Institutiones Theologicae Polemicae*, 5, 185-87.

Dicunt Doctores nostri Deum decrevisse Peccata non impedire, ut Decretum divinum Ratione Peccatorum se non habeat effective sed PERMISSIVE tantum. Idem notiscum Aug. conf. Fratres ubique asserunt.

Sed Difficultatem nondum solutam esse dicent Ecclesiæ Aug. Conf. Doctores, nec Dissensum hac Ratione sublatum, dum, quamdiu Reformati Decreti divini ratione Mediorum Salutis, Particularitatem Statuunt, Damnationis Culpa propter Gratiæ Denegationem nihilominus in Deum transferatur. Ita enim de hac re celeb. Buddeus "Caput vero totius Rei eo Redit, an Deus etiam illis qui damnantur Fidem et Constantiam in ea, largiri voluerit, si modo mediis Gratiæ rectæ uterenter? Hoc autem, cum nec Auctor Holzfusius nec reliqui hypothetici, adfirmare audeant, manifestum est eos Nubes et inania prensare, quando dicunt REPROBATIONIS Causam meritoriam esse Peccata hominum. Si enim Deus Fidem, et reliqua Gratiæ Dona, ut ex Peccatis eluctarentur, iis largiri noluit, revera Culpa Damnationis in Deum confertur, nec Reprobationis alia Causa est, quam absoluta ejus voluntas. Et hinc etiam patet, qua Pacto isti, Decretum Reprobationis absolutum esse negent." Hactenus vir Celebris. Ad quod regero.

1. Autorem dicere, Questionis totius summam huc redire, An deus illis etiam qui damnantur, Fidem et Constantiam in ea largiri voluerit, SI MODO MEDIIS GRATIæ RECTE UTEREUTUR? At undenam quæso est, ut hic vel ille mediis Gratiæ recte utatur, alius non recte utatur? undenam hoc Discrimen, bona hæc Hominis Dispositio? An Alium Autorem præter Deum agnoscit? A quo descendit omne Donum Bonum. A Quonam quæsio Omnes rerum Circumstantiæ, Occasiones Omnes, nisi a deo pendere possunt? si dicatur bonam hanc dispositionem, bonum hunc Gratiæ Usum Hominem a seipso habere, neque eum Deo acceptum ferendum esse, tunc aliquid Realitatis erit quod a deo non dependet, neque eum Autorem agnoscit. Si statuatur, bonam hanc Animi Hominis dispositionem, rectum hunc mediorum Gratiæ usum, favorabiles illas Circumstantias, bonam illam Educationem, mediis Gratiæ externis utendi Occasionem, Cordis Mollitiem, Deum Autorem habere, qui simul totius universi Autor est, omnis Existentiæ, omnis Rerum Nexus, omnis Modi et accidentis, ut et omnis Circumstantiæ; tunc nobiscum ultima Ratio cur hic vel ille dona Gratiæ sibi oblata accipiat, hic partinaciter resisat, alter non resistatat, in dei Gratia, vel ejus denegatione quærenda, ut Actus Præteritionis in Gratiæ Prævenientis denegatione consistat: si enim Deus voluisset, ut hic vel ille mediis Gratiæ recte uteretur, ipsius Animum, Circumstantias Occasionem, etiam aliter disposuisset

2. Si vero quæratur cur Deus huic vel illi donum contulerit, ut recte Mediis Gratiæ uterutur, alii non item, aliter responderi non potest quam deum nulli Peccatorum Gratiam suam conferre obligatum esse; Rationem vero cur nonnullis rectum mediorum Gratiæ usum donaverit, hanc esse, ut Gratiam suam ac misericordiam patefaceret: Cur vero hunc alteri prætulerit, huncque altero in favorabiliores posuerit Circumstantias, melius ipsi dederit Temperamentum, Cor tractabilius et quæ alia, hujus rei extra Dei Beneplacitum nulla inveniri potest Ratio; non magis ac si quæratur, cur Deus Personam Petri non fecerit Personam Judæ? Aut Cur Asiaticos non fecerit Europæos, vel cur spiritum hujus vel illius Angeli, vel potius hunc vel illum Angelum, non fecerit Animam hujus vell illius hominis? vel cur animam Petri, Pauli, vel hominis alius non fecerit Angelum, Cur huic potius homini quam alteri Temperamentum dederit aut Cholericum aut sanguineum? Cujus rei ultima Ratio nonnisi in Deo inveniri potest.

Stapferus, *Theologiæ Polemicæ*, Tom. V, pp. 203, etc.Ibid., *5*, 203-4.

Tenendum porro in hac doctrina multa esse impervestigabilia quorum Rationem reddere nemo mortalium possit; unde alii citius alii tardius ad βαδος illud Paulinum confugere coguntur, Exempla hujus Rei patebunt ex iis quæ nunc ex aliorum scriptis allegabimus

Asseram hujus Rei Testimonia ex scriptis celebrium doctorum Augustanæ Confessioni. Et primo quidem audiamus Reverendiss. Christ. Math. Pfaffium, hisce verbis, "Quodsi itaque omnia hæc, quæ hactenus dedimus de Gratia & Prædestinatione systemata, æqua Judicii Lance perpendamus, vix ullum occurret, quod ex omni Parte satisfaciat, et cui opponi nequeant Argumenta solvi nescia, ita ut ad voluntatem Dei absolutam cujus plane nullæ sint rationes, nemo fere statuerit, nisi Cartesius, paucissimique alii. Eam itaque intelligunt Theologi prudentiores, cujus sunt quidem sapientissimæ justissimæStapfer, p. 207, reads: "justissimæque." Rationes, ast nobis incognitæ, quia non revelatæ. Cæterum, Voluntatis dei absolutæ Termino nescio cur quidam adeo abhorreant cum Hulsemannus οπανυ eo utatur, seu eam cujus, non possunt dari Rationes, aut saltem ad εποχην et βαδος: illud Paulinum non sit recurrendum. scilicet sola hæc est differentia, quod ad hoc quidem extremum ii, qui Augustinum sequuntur, dominicani, Jansenistæ, Reformati, citius confugiant, cæteri tardius & nonnisi per Ambages: Arminiani, quamvis nec absolutam Baptismi vel Fidei in Christum explicitæ Necessitatem admittant, Adeoque non Opus fere habeant, ad Voluntatem dei absolutam et βαδος Paulinum recurrere, tum tamen ad eandem confugiunt Anchoram, ubi queritur, Cur deus tot Millibus hominum, qui si Evangelium audirent,*"* As the sinners of Tyre and Sidon, which Christ mentions, and innumerable others, which we may suppose with equal reason."-JE's note. salvarentur, id non annunciandum curet, cur Gratiam suam tam inequaliter distribuat et quibusdam eam largiatur tam copiosam, ut Miraculum foret, si resisterent, aliis non item; cur quosdam ex iis, qui sub finem vitæ resipiscunt, salvet, quosdam damnet, et quæ aliæ formari possunt Quæstiones huc pertinentes. Denique non errabimus, si dixerimus, et nobis quoque postremo ad liberam dei voluntatem, cujus Rationes nesciamus, utpote in Abysso scientiæ divinæ absconditas, esse deveniendum. scilicet cum supponamus universalem dei erga totum Genus humanumStapfer, 5, 207-08. eumque serium summunque, qui unquam cogirari potest, Amorem omnes æqualiter complectentem, meritum item Christi universale et vocationem universalem, hactenus cum grano salis accipiendam, quod multæ sint Nationes multæque Hominum Myriades, quæ Nihil unquam de Christo inaudivere, credamusStapfer reads: "credamusque." insimul, neminem nisi per externum Evangelii verbum salvari; affirmandumque denique nobis sit, Deum pro sapientia sua potuisse vel posse, omnes Homines, vel saltem quosdam salvere, si omnibus, vel saltem quibusdam illis alioqui pereuntibus vel perituris dedisset, vel daret eam Gratiæ Mensuram, qua data prævidisset singulos eos perseverantur credituros; oriuntur Quæstiones; Cur itaque deus quibusdam saltem eam Gratiæ dederit Mensuram, qua posita eos prævidit credituros, aliis eam saltem, qua posita prævidit non credituros, credituros autem, si majorem ipsis dedisset Gratiæ Gradum? Cur deus porro non omnibus annunciandum curet Evangelium, ejusque Lucem huic largiatur Nationi, aliam negligat, cum tamen ob Incredulitatem nemo possit damnari, nisi qui Evangelium audiverit? Cur Evangelio a duobus Populis rejecto ejusdem æqualisque ex omni Parte Peccati reis, hunc deus revocet repetita Evangelii Prædictione, alterum non item? Cur denique deus hunc Hominem in Peccato mortali perire sinat, alterum nihilo Meliorem ad Resipiscentiam revocet? Ad quæ quidem Quæsita ita respondent maximam Partem Nostrates, ut, cum de voluntate dei consequente maxime hic agi putent, ad liberum dei Arbitrium tandem recurrant, et βαδος Consiliorum divinorum impenetrabile. Id quod cum Reformatis subinde objiciat Hulsemannus affirmans eos, quando se expedire non possint, vociferari: O Altitudo sane et nobis, maxime in Argumento, de varia et inæquali mediorum Salutis dispensatione, deque ejusdem cum Benevolentia dei universali conciliandæ Ratione, usu sæpius venit ipso affirmante Hulsemanno ita, ut cum Apostolo Paulo fateri habeamus necesse, nos Judiciorum Voluntatisque divinæ Rationes ignorare, in quo utique tandem omnium Ratiocinationum nostrarum constituitur Terminus." Hactenus Ven. Pfaffius. Afferamus adhuc Puffendorfii de hac re Judicium. "Deinde et id nemo sanus negaverit, in hoc Negotio dari Quæstiones, Quarum Rationes ab hominibus investigari nequeant, et ad quas Nihil aliud restet, quam exclamare, O Altitudo! V. Gr. Quare deus Tempora Ignorantiæ tamdiu dissimulaverit?Stapfer reads: "17." Acts 16:30. Romans 16:25. Ephesians 3:5. Colossians 1:26. Quare alicui Populo citius, alicui tardius Doctrinam salutis annuntiari fecerit? Acts 16:6-7. Cum plurimi Populi Americani, multi in reconditis Partibus Africæ, et Asiæ, invincibili Evangelii Ignorantia laborent, quæ Ratio sit quare hi damnentur? Et quæ aliæ paris Indolis Quæstiones formari possunt." Id unicum adhuc monemus, quod, licet tandem ad dei, Beneplacitum confugiatur, minime tamen statuatur deum per ipsum hoc Beneplacitum non secundum summam suam sapientiam agere, cum nihil velit nisi respectu habito omnium suorum Attributorum. Cum ergo deus semper agat vi sapientiæ suæ, quæ consideratur ceu Norma quædam omnium suorum Attributorum omniumque Actionum, sequitur: sapientissimas ipsi semper esse Rationes cur potius hoc quam aliud velit, ut semper respiciat Rerum Convenientias, neque quidquam faciat quod fini universi generali non est convenientissimum, licet Rationes illæ propter Intellectus nostri Limites plerumque nobis sint incognitæ. Ex eo vero quod deus aliquid voluit, semper concludere possumus illud etiam optimum esse, unde Rationem propter, quam aliquid optimum esse censemus a dei voluntate desumimus, et quærenti cur hanc Rerum seriem optimam esse arbitremur, respondemus quia Deus eam elegit, sive voluit.

Stapferus, *Theologiæ Polemicæ*, Tom. V, pp. 208, etc.Stapfer, 5, 208 ff.

It can be made evident by reason, that nothing can come to pass, but what it is the will and pleasure of God that it should come to pass. This may be argued:

1. From the infinite happiness of God. For every being had rather things should go according to his will, than not. Because if he had not rather, then 'tis not his will. It is a contradiction to say he wills it, and yet don't choose it, or had not rather it should be so, than not.

But if God had rather things should be according to his will than not, then, if the thing falls out otherwise than he hath willed,MS: "willed then he." he meets with a cross; because, by the supposition, he had rather it should have been otherwise, and therefore he would have been better pleased if the thing had been otherwise. 'Tis contrary to what he chose, and therefore it is of necessity that he must be displeased; 'tis of necessity that every being should be pleased when a thing is as he chooses, or had rather it should be. 'Tis a contradiction to suppose otherwise, for 'tis the very notion of being pleased, to have things agreeable to one's pleasure. For the very same reason, every being is crossed, or it is unpleasing to him, when a thing is that he chose, and had rather should not have been: for that is the very notion of a thing being cross or unpleasing to any, that 'tis contrary to his pleasure.

But if God can meet with crosses and things unpleasing to him, then he is not perfectly and unchangeably happy, for to be unpleasing and unpleasant are the same thing: for wherever there is any unpleasedness or unpleasantness, it must of necessity in a degree diminish his happiness. Where there is any cross to a being's choice, there is something contrary to happiness; wherever there is any unpleasedness, there is something contrary to pleasure, and which consequently diminishes pleasure. 'Tis impossible anything should be plainer than this.

The commands and prohibitions of God are only significations of our duty and his nature. 'Tis acknowledged that sin is, in itself considered, infinitely contrary to God's nature; but it does not follow but that it may be the pleasure of God to permit it, or that it should be agreeable to his will that it should be permitted that it might be notwithstanding, for the sake of the good that he will bring out of it. God can bring that good out of that which, in itself, is contrary to hisMS: "the." JE Jr.'s insert. nature, and which, in itself considered, he abhors; which good may be agreeable. And when itJE Jr. changes this word to "sin." is spoken of as contrary to the will of God, it is contrary to his will only considered as in itself, and by itself.JE Jr. deletes: "& by it self." As man commits it, it is contrary to God's will: for man's act in committing it, is limited by the evil that is in it. But as God permits it, it is not contrary to God's will: for God, in permitting it, has respect to the great good that he will make it an occasion of. If God respects sin, or considers it as man respects it in committing of it, it would be exceeding contrary to his will. But considering it as God decrees to permit it, 'tis not contrary to God's will.

To give an instance: the crucifying of Christ was a great sin, and as men committed it, it was exceeding hateful and highly provoking to God. And yet, upon many great considerations, it was the will of God that it should be done. Will anybody say that it was not the will of God that Christ should be crucified? Acts 4:28.

Sin is an evil, yet the futurition of sin, or that sin should be future, is not an evil thing. Evil is an evil thing, and yet it may be a good thing that evil should be in the world. There is certainly a difference between the thing itself being, and its being an evil thing, that ever it came to pass: as, for instance, it might be an evil thing to crucify Christ, but yet it was a good thing that crucifying Christ came to pass. As man's act, it was evil; but as God ordered it, it was good. Who will deny but that it may be so, that evil's coming to pass may be an occasion of a greater good than that is an evil, and so of there being more good in the whole, than if that evil had not come to pass? And if so, then it is a good thing that that evil comes to pass. When we say, the thing is an evil thing in itself, then we mean that it is evil, considering of it only within its own bounds.

But when we say that 'tis a good thing that ever it came to pass, then we consider the thing as a thing among events, or as one thing belonging to the series of events, and as related to the rest of the series.

AsJE Jr. inserts and then deletes: "* No. 22," and deletes "as." if a man should say that it was a good thing that ever it happened that Joseph's brethren sold him into Egypt; or that it was a good thing that ever it came to pass, Pope {Leo X}JE Jr.'s insert where JE drew a dash. sent out indulgences for the commission of future sins: nobody would understand a man as thus expressing himself, as justifying these acts.

It implies no contradiction to suppose that an act may be an evil act, and yet that 'tis a good thing that such an act should come to pass. A man may have been a bad man, and yet the case may be so, that it may have been a good thing that there has been such a man. This implies no contradiction, because it implies no contradiction to suppose that there being such a man, may be an occasion of there being more good in the whole, than there would have been otherwise. So itJE Jr. deletes this word. will no more implyMS: "it is." a contradiction to suppose that an action may be a bad action, and yet it be a good thing that there has been such an action.

Their objection against this will be, that I plead that God may do evil, that good may come.

God's commands and calls and counsels do imply another thing, viz. that 'tis our duty to endeavor these things, and that it may be, and yet be certain beforehand that we shall not do them. To give instances.

AndHere the argument is resumed from three paragraphs up, ending "Acts 4:28." if there be any difficulty in this,I.e. in God's permitting sin. the same difficulty will attend their own scheme: for they allow that God permits sin, and therefore, as he permits it, it cannot be contrary to his will. For if it were contrary to his will, as he permits it, then it would be contrary to his will to permit: for that is same thing. But nobody will say that God permits sin, when it is against his will to permit it: for this would be to make him act involuntarily, or against his own will.JE inserts a cue to subsection 31 both here and at the end of the next passage, relocated by JE's direction from subsections 17-19, at the end of which he refers to the same subsection 31. Therefore, the passage from subsection 31 has been relocated below, following the end of the relocated text ending in subsection 19 (JE Jr. directs the same in his cues).

2.This passage resumes the argument above, paragraph ending "Acts 4:28." It may be argued from the infinite power and wisdom of God, that nothing can come to pass, but that it must be agreeable to the will and pleasure of God that it should come to pass.

For as was observed before, every being had rather things should be according to his will than not; and therefore, if things ben't according to his will, it must be for want of power. It can't be for want of will, by supposition; it must therefore be for want of sufficiency. It must be either because he can't have it so, or can't have it so without some difficulty, or some inconvenience. All may be expressed in a word, viz. that he wants sufficiency to have things as he would. But this cannot be the case of a being of infinite power and infinite wisdom: if he has infinite power and wisdom, he can order all things to be just as he would, and he can order it with perfect, infinite ease, or without the least difficulty or inconveniency. There are two things lie before him,JE Jr.'s insert. both equally within his power: either to order the matter to be so, or not order 'em to be so, and both of them equally easy to him. One is as little trouble to him as the other; as to easiness or trouble, they are perfectly equal; 'tis asMS: "as to." easy to him to order it as not to order it. And therefore his determination in this case, whether ordering of it or not ordering of it, must be a certain sign of his will in the case, whether he would have the thing so or not so: i.e. if he does order it to be so, that is a sign his will is that it should be so; and if he don't order it to be so, but suffers it to be otherwise, that is as sure a sign that he wills that it should be otherwise: so that however the thing is, it is a sure sign that 'tis the will of God that it should be as it is.

And therefore, when God suffers some men to fail of performing the condition of the covenant of grace, 'tis a certain sign that it was his pleasure that this should come to pass.JE draws a large "X" through this paragraph.

To this nothing can be said, but that 'tis not for want of will nor want of power in God, that things ben't as he would have them; but because the nature of the subject won't allow of it. But how can this be to the purpose, when the nature of the subject itself is of God, and is wholly within his power, is altogether the fruit of his mere will: and cannot a God of infinite wisdom and infinite power cause the natures of things to be such, and order them so after they are crossed, as to have things as he would, or without his will being crossed, and things coming to pass so as he had rather have them otherwise?

As, for instance, God foresaw who would comply with the terms of salvation, and who would not; and he could have forborne to give being to such as he foresaw would not, if it had not been so that,MS: "that he." upon some consideration, it was his pleasure that there should be some that should not comply with the terms of salvation.

Particularly they may say, God can't always prevent men's sins, unless he acts contrary to the free nature of the subject, or without destroying men's liberty.

But will they deny that an omnipresent and infinitely wise God, could not possibly invent and set before menJE Jr.'s insert. such strong motives to obedience, and have kept them before them in such a manner as should have influenced all mankind to have continued in their obedience, as the elect angels have done, without destroying their liberty? God well orders it, so that the saints and angels in heaven never will sin: and does it therefore follow that their liberty is destroyed, and that they ben't free, but forced, in their actions? Does it follow that they are turned into blocks, as the Arminians say the Calvinists' doctrine turns men?

"The wrath of man shall praise thee, and the remainder of that wrath wilt thou restrain" Psalms 76:10. If God restrains sin when he pleases, and when he permits it, permits it for the sake of some good that it will be an occasion of, and does actually restrain it in all other cases: 'tis evident that when he permits it, it is his will that it should come to pass for the sake of that good that it will be an occasion of; if he permits it for the sake of that good, then he don't permit it merely because he should infringe on the creature's liberty in restraining it: as is farther evident, because he does restrain it when that good is not in view.

If it be His will to permit it to come to pass for the sake of the good that its coming to pass will be an occasion of, then 'tis his will to permit it, that by its coming to pass he may obtain that good; and therefore it must necessarily be his will that it should come to pass, that he may obtain that good. If he permits it, that by its coming he may obtain such a good, then his proximate end in permitting it, is that it may come to pass. And if he wills the means for the sake of the end, he therein wills the end.

If God wills to permit a thing, that it may come to pass, then he wills that it should come to pass. 'Tis self-evident. But if he wills to permit it to come to pass, that by its coming to pass he may obtain some end, then he wills to permit it, that it should come to pass: for to will to permit a thing to come to pass, that by its coming to pass good may be obtained, is the same thing exactly as to will to permit it to come to pass, that it may come to pass, and so the end may be obtained. To will to permit a thing to come to pass, that he may obtain some end by its coming to pass, and yet to be unwilling that it should come to pass, certainly implies a contradiction.

See Doddridge's note on Acts 18:10.Doddridge, *The Family Expositor*; *or, a Paraphrase and Version of the New Testament* (1836 ed.), p. 451. In his commentary on Acts 18:10, Doddridge argues that the expression "people in this city" cannot refer to virtuous heathen but only those who would be converted to Christianity.

Here I confess that God decrees good and evil in a different manner: viz. 1. that he decrees that he will cause the good; 2. that he will permit the evil. But yet that God decrees all the good that ever comes to pass, and therefore there certainly will come to pass no more good than he has absolutely decreed to cause. And therefore, there certainly and infallibly will no more believe, no more be godly, and no more be saved, than God has decreed that he will cause to believe, and cause to be godly, and will save.

The foreknowledge of God will necessarily infer a decree, for God could not foreknow that they would be, unless he had decreed they should be, and that, because they would not be future, unless he had decreed they should be. If God from all eternity knew that such and such things were future, then they were future; then the proposition from all eternity was true, that such a thing, at such a time, should be; and it is as much impossible that a thing should be future, without some reason of its being future, as that it should actually be, without some reason why it is. It is as unreasonable to suppose that this proposition should be true—viz. such a thing will be, or is to be, without a reason why it is true—as 'tis that this proposition should be true: "Such a thing actually is, or has been, without some reason why that is true." For as the being of the thing is not in its own nature necessary,MS: "necessary &." so that proposition that was true before—viz. that it shall be—is not in its own nature a necessary truth. And therefore, I draw this consequence: That if there must be some reason of the futurition of the thing, or why the thing is future, this can be no other than God's decree; or, the truth of the proposition, "Such a thing will be," has been determined by God. For the truth of the proposition is determined by the supposition. My meaning is, that it don't remain a question, but the matter is decided whether the proposition shall be true or not.

The thing in its own nature is not necessary, but only possible; and therefore 'tis not of itself that it is future, and 'tis not of itself in a state of futurition—if I may so speak—but only in a state of possibility. There must be something to bring it out of a state of mere possibility, into a state of futurition, and this must be God only: for there was no other being, by supposition, existing. And though other things are future, yet it won't be sufficient to say that the futurition of other things, is the cause of the futurition of this. And 'tis owing only to him that is the First Being, and that exists necessarily and of himself, that all other things that are not in their own nature necessary, or necessarily future but merely possibility, are brought out of that state of mere possibility into a state of futurition, to be certainly future.

Here is an effect already done, viz. the rendering that which, in its own nature, is only possible, to be certainly future, so that it can be certainly known to be so; and there must be something already existing that must have caused this effect.

Whatsoever is not of itself, or by the necessity of its own nature, is an effect of something else; but that such a thing should be future, by supposition, is not of itself or by necessity of its own nature.

If things that appertain to the creature, or things that come to pass in time, ben't future of themselves and of their own nature, then they are future, because God makes them to be future. 'Tis exceeding evident, for there is nothing else at at all, but only God, and things that come to pass in time; and therefore, if things that come to pass in time han't the reason of their own futurition in themselves, it must be in God: for it must be either in God, or themselves, for there is nothing else at all but these two.

But if you say that the ground or reason of their futurition, is in the things themselves, then things are future prior to any decree, or their futurition is antecedent in nature to any decree of God: and then to what purpose is any decree of God at all? For according to this supposition, God's decreeing does not make anything future, nor not future, because it was future prior to his decree; his decreeing or appointing that anything shall be, or shall not be, don't alter the case. 'Tis not to be, nor not to be anything, the more for God's decreeing of it. According to this supposition, God has no freedom or choice in decreeing or appointing anything—at least according to the Arminian notion of liberty. 'Tis not at all His choice what shall be future, and what not, no, not in one thing: for the futurition of things is, by their supposition, antecedent or prior in nature to his choice, so that his choosing or refusing don't alter the case. The things in themselves are to be future, and his decreeing can't make 'em not future: for they can't be future and not future at the same time; neither can it make 'em future, because they were future already. So that in pleading up for man's liberty, they destroy the freedom of God himself, according to their own principles.

'Tis allowed that things are future before they come to pass, because God foreknows either things are future antecedent to God's decree, and independent of it, or they are not. If they are not future antecedent to and independent of God's decree, then they are made so by his decree; there is no medium. But if they are so antecedent of his decree, then the above-mentioned absurdity will follow, viz. that God has no power by his decree to make anything future or not future; he has no choice in the case.

And if it be already decided, something must have decided; for it has been already shown, 'tis not true without a reason why it is true. If something has determined or decided the truth of it, it must be God that must have decided it, or something else. It can't be chance, or mere accident; that is contrary to the supposition: for 'tis supposed that there is some reason for it, and that something DOES decide it. If there be anything that comes to pass by mere accident, then that comes to pass of itself, without any reason. If it ben't chance, therefore, that has decided it, it must be God or the creature. It can't be the creature, as actually existing, for by supposition this is before any, from all eternity, before any creature exists; therefore, if it be anything in the creature that decides it any way, it must be only the futurition of that thing in the creature. But this only brings the same question back again, viz. "What determines the futurition of that thing that decides?" And if it be answered, "The futurition of some other thing," then the question recurs, "What decided the futurition of that thing?," till we must necessarily come at last—let us put if off as long as we will—to the First Being as determining the futurition of all things. It may be illustrated by a comparison made use of in another case by a certain author, as if there were a chain hung down out of the heavens to the earth, and it should be inquired, "What held up that chain?," and it should be answered that one link was held up by the next, and that by the next, and so on. But this don't resolve the question: for still the question remains to be solved, viz. "What holds up the whole chain?"

To suppose that God's decrees are conditional in the sense of the Arminians, or that they depend, as they suppose, on a foresight of something that shall come to pass in time, is to suppose that something that first begins to be in time, is the cause of something that has been from all eternity; which is absurd, for nothing can be a cause of that existence which is before the existence of that cause. What an absurdity is it, to suppose that that existence, which is the effect, is effected by a cause, when the cause that effects it is not, has no being. If it be answered, that 'tis not the actual existence of the thing that is the reason or cause of the decree, but the foresight of the existence; the foresight of the existence may be at the same time with the decree, and before it in the order of nature, though the existence itself is not; and that 'tis not properly the actual existence of the thing foreseen that is the cause of the decree, but the existence of it in the divine foreknowledge: I answer, that this don't help the difficulty at all, but only puts it a step further off; for still, by their scheme, the foreknowledge depends on the future actual existence, so that the actual existence is the cause of the divine foreknowledge, which is infinite ages before it. And 'tis as great an absurdity to suppose this effect to flow from this cause before the existence of the cause; and whatever is said, the absurdity will recur unless we suppose that the divine decree is the ground of the futurition of the events, and also the ground of the foreknowledge of it: then the cause is before the effect, but otherwise the effect is before the cause.

To argue it from the immutability of God. To show how many things there must be now in God.

1. Their first objection is, that it infringes on the creatures' liberty. In answer toJE Jr.'s insert. this objection, and to show first, what is the true notion of liberty; and secondly, the absurdity of their notion of liberty.The location of the following seven paragraphs, a string of passages from secs. 10, 33, and 35 connected by JE's cues, follows JE, Jr.'s direction, and therefore is conjectural.

Their notion of liberty, is that there is a sovereignty in the will, and that the will determines itself, so that its determination to choose or refuse this or that, should be primarily within itself. Which description of liberty implies a self-contradiction: for it supposes the will, in its first act of choosing or refusing, to be determined by itself, which implies that there is an antecedent act of the will to that first act, determining of it. For if the will determines its own first act, then there must be an act of the will before that first act, for that determining is acting; which is a contradiction. There can be no fallacy in this, for we know that if the will, etc.

If the will determines its own acts, it don't determine it without acting. And therefore, here is this contradiction, viz. that there is an act of the will before the first act; there is an act of the will determining what it shall choose before the first act of choice. Which is as much as to say that there is an act of volition in this case, before the first act of volition: for the will's determining what it will choose, is choosing; the will's determining what it will will, is willing. So that, according to this notion of liberty, the will must choose before it chooses, in order to determine what it will choose.

If the will determines itself, it is certain that one act must determine another. If the will determines its own choice, then it must determine by a foregoing act what it will choose. If the will determines its own act, then an antecedent act determines the consequent, for that determining is acting; the will can't determine without acting. Therefore I inquire, What determines that first act of the will, viz. its determining of its own act? It must be answered, according to their scheme, that 'tis the will, by a foregoing act; and here again we come to the same contradiction, viz. that the first act of the will is determined by an act that is before that first act.

If the will determines itself, or determines its own choice, the meaning of it must be—if there be any meaning belonging to it—that the will determines how it will choose, and that it chooses, according to that, its own determination how to choose, or is directed in choosing by that its own determination. But then I would inquire, Whether that first determination that directs the choiceMS: "Choice be." itself ben't an act or a volition; and if so, I would inquire, What determines that act to be this way or that? Is it another determination, or a determination still prior to that in the order of nature? Then I would inquire, What determines the first act or determination of all?

If the will, in its acts of willing or choosing, determines or directs itself how to choose, then there is something done by the will besides, prior to its act of choosing, that is determined, viz. its determining or directing itself how to choose. This act's determining or directing, must be something besides or distinct from the choice determined or directed, and must be prior in order of nature to it. Here are two acts of the will, one the cause of the other, viz. the act of the will directing and determining, and the act or choice directed or determined. Now I would inquire, What determines that first act of the will determining, or directing to determine and direct, as it does? If it be said, the will determines itself in that, then that supposes that there is another act of the will, prior to that directing and determining that act, which is contrary to the supposition; and if it was not, still the question would return, What determines that first determining act of the will?

If the will determines itself, one of these three must be meant, viz.: 1. That that very same act of the will determines itself. But this is as absurd as to say that something makes itself, and it supposes it to be before it is: for the act of determining, is as much prior to the thing determined, as the act making is before the thing made. Or, 2. The meaning must be that the will determines its own act by some other act that is prior to it in the order of nature, which is contrary to the supposition of the will being determined by itself, even in its own first act. Or, 3. The meaning must be that the faculty, considered as hitherto perfectly without act, determines its own consequent act; which is talk without a meaning, and a great absurdity. To suppose that the faculty remaining hitherto perfectly without act, can determine anything, isMS: "it." is a plain contradiction: for determining is acting.
