And besides, if the will does determine itself, that power of determining itself don't argue any freedom, unless it be by an act of the will, or unless that determination how it will, be itself an act of choice: for what freedom or liberty is there in the will determining itself, without an act of choice in determining, whereby it may choose which way it will determine itself? So that those that suppose the will has a power of self-determination, must suppose that that very determination is an act of the will, or an act of choice, or else it don't at all help 'em out in what they would, viz. the liberty of the will. But if that very determination how to act,MS: "act be." itself be an act of choice, then the question returns, What determines that act of choice?

And also, the foreknowledge of God contradicts their notion of liberty as much, and in every respect, just in the same manner as a decree. For they don't pretend that aMS: "an." decree contradicts liberty, any otherwise than as it infers that 'tis beforehand certain that the thing will come to pass, and impossible but that it should be as the decree makes an indissoluble connection beforehand between the subject and predicate of the proposition, viz. that such a thing shall be. A decree infers no other necessity than that. And God's foreknowledge does infer the same, to all intents and purposes: for if from all eternity GodJE Jr.'s insert. foreknew that such a thing would be, then the event was infallibly certain beforehand, and that proposition was true from all eternity, that such a thing should be; and therefore, there was an indissoluble connection beforehand between the subject and predicate of that proposition. If the proposition was true beforehand, the subject and predicate of it were connected beforehand; and therefore it follows from hence that it is utterly impossible that it should not prove true, and that for this reason: because 'tis utterly impossible that a thing should be true and not true at the same time. To illustrate this by the example of Judas.

How unfair therefore is it, in those that hold the foreknowledge of God, to insist upon this objection from human liberty against the decrees, when the same objection is attended with the same difficulty, exactly in the same manner.

2. Their other objection is that God's decrees make God the author of sin. I answer, that there is no more necessity of supposing God the author of sin on this scheme, than the other: for if we suppose, according to my doctrine, that God has determined from all eternity the number and persons of those that shall perform the condition of the covenant of grace, in order to support this doctrine there is no need of maintaining any more concerning God's decreeing sin than this, viz. that God has decreed that he will permit all the sin that ever comes to pass, and that upon his permitting it, it will certainly come to pass. And they hold the same thing; for they hold that God does determine beforehand to permit all the sin that does come to pass, and that he certainly knows that if he does permit it, itJE Jr.'s insert. will come to pass. I say, they in their scheme allow both these: they allow that God does permit all the sin to come to pass that ever does come to pass, and those that allow the foreknowledge of God do also allow the other thing, viz. that he knows, concerning all the sin that ever does really come to pass, that it will come to pass upon his permitting it. So that if this be making God the author of sin, they make him so in the very same way that they charge us with doing it.

One objection of theirs against God's decreeing or ordering in any sense that sin should come to pass, is that man cannot do this without making himself sinful, and in some measure guilty of the sin, and that therefore God cannot. To this I answer, that the same objection lies against their own scheme, two ways:

1. Because they own that God does permit sin, and that he determines to permit it beforehand, and that he knows, with respect to all sin that ever is committed, that upon his permitting it, it will come to pass; and we hold no other.

2. Their objection is, that what is a sin in man, is a sin in God, and therefore in any sense to decree sin, would be a sin. But if this objection be good, 'tis as strong against God's permission of sin, which they allow: for it would be a sin in God, even to permit sin. We ought not to permit or suffer it, where we have opportunity to hinder it, and can't do it without making ourselves in some measure guilty. But yet they allow that God does permit sin, and that his permitting it don't make him guilty of it. Why must the argument from men to God be stronger in the other case, than in this?

The same kind of infallible certainty that the thing will come to pass, or impossibility but that it should, that they object against, must necessarily be inferred another way, whether we hold the thing to be any way decreed or not: for it has been shown before, and I suppose none will deny, that God from all eternity decrees his own actions, and therefore he from all eternity decrees every punishment. So that 'tis impossible, by their own reasoning, but that the punishment should come to pass; and if it be impossible but that the punishment should come to pass, then it is equally impossible but that the sin should come to pass. For if it be possible that the sin should not come to pass, and yet impossible but that the punishment should come to pass, then 'tis impossible but that God should punish the sin that may never be.

For God certainly to know that a thing will beJE Jr.'s insert. that possibly may be, and possibly may not be, implies a contradiction. If possibly it may be otherwise, then how can God know certainly that it will be? If it possibly may be otherwise, then he knows it possibly may be otherwise, and that is inconsistent with his certainly knowing that it will not be otherwise. If God certainly knows it will be, and yet it may possibly be otherwise, then it may possibly happen to be otherwise than God certainly knows it will be; and if so, then it may possibly happen that God may be mistaken in his judgment, when he certainly knows: for 'tis supposed, it is so possible, that it should be otherwise than he judges. For it to be otherwise than he judges, and for him to be mistaken, are the same thing.

They say that we ought to begin in religion with the perfections of God, and make these a rule to interpret Scripture. *Answ*. 1. If this be the best rule, I ask, Why is it not as good a rule to argue from these perfections of God—his omniscience, infinite happiness, infinite wisdom and power—as his other attributes that they argue from? If it ben't as good a rule to argue from these as those, it must be because they ben't so certain, or it is not so certain.JE Jr. inserts: "that he is possessed of these perfections." But this they will not maintain, for his moral perfections are proved no otherwise than by arguing from his natural perfections; and therefore they must be equally certain with them. What we prove another thing by, must at least be as certain as it makes the thing proved by it. And if an absolute and universal decree does infer a seeming inconsistence with some of God's perfections, they must confess the contrary to have a seeming inconsistence with the natural perfections of God. Again,

[*Answ.*] 2. They lay it down for a rule, to embrace no doctrine they by their own reason can't reconcile with the moral perfections of God. But I would show the unreasonableness of this rule. For 1. if this be a good rule, then it always was so—and then let us see what will follow. Then we have reason to conclude everything to be really inconsistent with God's moral perfections that we can't reconcile with his moral perfections: for if we han't reason to conclude that 'tis inconsistent, then we have no reason to conclude that it is not true. But if this be true—that we have reason to conclude everything is inconsistent {with God's moral perfections},The supplied phrases in curly brackets in the remainder of the paragraph are provided by JE Jr. where JE drew dashes. that we can't reconcile {with those perfections}—then David had reason to conclude some things that he saw were inconsistent {with God's moral perfections}, for he could not reconcile {them with those perfections}; then Job had cause {to come to the same conclusion concerning some events in his day}.

[*Answ.*] 3. If it be a good rule, that we must conclude that to be inconsistent with the divine perfections that we can't reconcile with {those perfections}—or, which is the same thing, that we can't see how it is consistent with themJE Jr. inserts: "those perfections."—then it must be because we have reason to conclude that that cannot be, that our reason cannot see how it can be; and then it will follow that we must reject the doctrine of the Trinity, etc.

The Scripture itself supposes that there are some things in the scriptures that men may not be able to reconcile with God's moral perfections, as in that 9th of Romans v. 19: "Why doth he yet find fault? For who hath resisted his will?" And the Apostle don't answer the objection by showing of us how to reconcile it {with the moral perfections of God},JE Jr.'s insert where JE drew a dash. but by rebuking the arrogancy of quarrelling with revealed doctrines under such a pretense, and not considering the infinite distance between God and us: "Nay, but who art thou, O man, that repliest against God?" v. 20. And God answered Job after the same manner: God rebuked him for darkening counsel by words without knowledge Job 38:2, and answered him only by declaring and manifesting to him the infinite distance between God and him, in so letting of him know that it became him humbly to submit to Him and acknowledge His justice, even in those things that there were difficult to his reason, and that without solving his difficulties any other way, than by making of him sensible of the weakness of his own understanding.

In the beginning of the discourse, argue from reason about it. First, to prove the doctrine of God's decrees from reason; and secondly, to answer their objections from reason, and then to proceed to the argument from Scripture.

To answer their objection from the truth of God, and to show that this scheme labors under no greater difficulty in that respect than theirs.

Vid. under the title of Universal Redemption, p. 000.MS: "No. 2."

To conclude the discourse with showing the unreasonableness of rejecting plain revelations, because they are puzzling to our reason.

If there were difficulties which their own scheme does not labor with, which yet there is not one .. .

So that though the doctrine of the decree be mysterious and attended with difficulties, yet if the Scripture had made no revelation about it, the opposite doctrine is in itself more mysterious and attended with greater difficulties, more evident contradictions to reason, to one that thoroughly considers things. So that if the Scripture had made no revelation about it, we should have had most reason to have believed; but when the Scripture is so abundant .. .JE draws a cue mark and writes a reference to "23," but there is no corresponding mark in that section nor anywhere else in the MS.

In the first place, I lay it down—which I suppose none will deny-that, as to God's own actions, God decrees 'em, or purposes 'em beforehand: for none will be so absurd as to say that God acts without intention, or without designingMS: "designing or." to act, or that he forbears to act without intending to forbear.

Secondly, that whatsoever God intends or purposes, he intends and purposes from all eternity, and that there are no new purposes or intentions in God. For if God sometime begins to intend what he did not intend before, then two things will follow:

1. That God is not omniscient. If God sometimes begins to design what he did notJE Jr.'s insert. design before, it must of necessity be for want of knowledge, or for want of knowing things before as he knows them now, for want of having exactly the same views of things. If God begins to intend what he did not before intend, it must be because he now sees reasons to intend it, that he did not see before, or that he has something new objected to his understanding to influence him.

2. If God begins to intend or purpose things that he did not intend before, then God is certainly mutable, then he must in his own mind and will be liable to succession and change: for wherever there are new things, there is succession and change. Therefore, I shall take these two things for things granted and supposed in this controversy, viz.: that as to God's own actions or forbearings to act, he decrees and purposes 'em beforehand; and that whatsoever God designs or purposes, he purposes from all eternity, and so that he decrees all his own actions, or forbearings to act, from all eternity.

*Corol*. Hence God decrees from all eternity to permit all the evil that ever he does permit, because God's permitting is God's forbearing to act or to prevent.

There is no one difficulty in this my doctrine that is avoided in their scheme, but only they are put a little further out of sight.

"The election hath obtained, and the rest were blinded" Romans 11:7. ThatJE Jr.'s insert. by "the election"JE Jr. underlines "the Election," inserts: "here." is not meant the Gentiles, but the elect part of the Jews, is most apparent by the context.

ALL THAT ARE SAVED ARE ELECTED. Vid. clause above, and following clauses.

If they allow that some only are elected, and not all that are saved, and so none reprobated, then they overthrow hereby their own main objection against reprobation, viz. that God offers salvation to all, and encourages them to seek it; whichJE Jr. inserts: "say they." would be inconsistent with God's truth, if he had absolutely determined not to save them: for they won't deny that those that are elected, whilst ungodly, are warned of God to beware of eternal damnation,MS: "salvation." JE Jr.'s change. and to avoid such things, lest they should be damned. But for God to warn men to beware of damnation, though he has absolutely determined that they shall not be damned, is exactly parallel with his exhorting men to seek salvation, though he has actually determined that they shall not be saved.

GOD DECREES ALL THINGS, AND EVEN SINS. Acts 2:28; Acts 2:23, "Him, being delivered by the determinate counsel and foreknowledge of God"; Acts 4:28, "to do whatsoever thy hand and thy counsel determined before to be done." If one thing meant, be only that Christ's sufferings should come to pass by some means or other, I answer, They could not come to pass but by sin, for contempt and disgrace was one thing he was to suffer.

Even the free actions of men are subject to God's disposal. Proverbs 21:1, "The king's heart is in the hand of the Lord, he turns it as the rivers of water whithersoever it pleaseth him."

________________________

GOD'S DECREES AS THEY RESPECT SIN. See Jeremiah 52:3, "For through the anger of the Lord it came to pass on Jerusalem and Judah, till he had cast them out from his presence, that Zedekiah rebelled against the king of Babylon."

DECREEING SIN. Jeremiah 52:3, "For through the anger of the Lord it came to pass. .. that Zedekiah rebelled against the king of Babylon." See also 2 Kings 24:20; see back, p. 00;MS: "p. 43," which, following JE's direction, is inserted as the entry immediately preceding. Joshua 2:23 and Joshua 3:1-2, 4, with other places where the not exalting the nations is attributed to the people's sinful negligence.

NotJE Jr. inserts "The" before this word. complying with the terms of the covenant of grace. 1 Peter 2:8, "a stone of stumbling, and a rock of offense, to them that stumble at the word, being disobedient: whereunto also they were appointed."

What man determines never comes to pass, unless God determines it. Lamentations 3:37, "Who is he that saith, and it cometh to pass, and the Lord commandeth it not?" By command it is here meant "willing"; and God is elsewhere said "to speak, and it was done; to command, and it stood fast" Psalms 33:9.

God determines the limits of men's lives. This is exceeding evident, Job 7:1. "Days of an hireling" signifies an appointed, certain, limited time, as Isaiah 16:14 and Isaiah 21:16. To show that if the limits of men's lives are determined, men's free actions must be determined, and even their sins, for this often depends on such acts. See also, Job 14:5.

ELECTION NOT FROM A FORESIGHT OF WORKS, or conditional, or depending on the condition of men's wills, as is evident by 2 Timothy 1:9, "Who hath saved us, and called us with an holy calling, not according to our works, but according to his own purpose and grace, which was given us in Christ Jesus before the world began"; Philippians 2:13, "For 'tis God that worketh in you both to will and to do of his own good pleasure"; Romans 9:15-16, "I will have mercy on whom I will have mercy,. .. So then 'tis not of him that willeth, nor of him that runneth, but of God that showeth mercy."

Men's labors and endeavors themselves are from God. 1 Corinthians 15:10, "But by the grace of God I am what I am: and his grace which was bestowed upon me was not in vain; but I labored more abundantly than they all: yet not I, but the grace of God which was with me."

If there be no election, then 'tis not God that makes men to differ, expressly contrary to Scripture. No man ought to praise God for that happiness that he has above other men, or for that distinction there is between him and other men; that he is holy and that he is saved, when they are not holy. The saints in heaven, when they look on the devils in hell, would have no occasion to praise on account of the difference between them.JE Jr.'s insert.

To mention this, of one not having cause to praise and love God for thus distinguishing us, under the application, showing the ill consequences of theJE Jr. inserts: "Arminian." doctrine, howMS: "any how." it robs God of great part of the glory of his grace, and takes away a principal motive to love and praise, and exalts men in God's room and ascribes the glory to self that belongs alone to God.

FOREKNOWLEDGE OF GOD. If God don't know all things, then his knowledge may increase. He may gain, and may grow wiser, as he grows older. He may discover new things, and may draw consequences from them.

And he may be mistaken. If he don't know, he may guess wrong. If he don't know, he has no infallible judgment, for an infallible judgment is knowledge.

And if heJE Jr.'s insert. may be mistaken, he may order matters wrong. He may be frustrated; his measures may be broken. For doubtless, in things that are uncertain, he orders things according to what appears most probable, or else he fails in prudence; but in so ordering things, his measures may be broken.

And then the greater part of the greater events, viz. events among rational creatures, would be uncertain to him: for the greater part of them depend on men's free actions.

And then, that he does foreknow, is evident by his predicting, foretelling events,JE Jr.'s insert. and even the sins of men, as Judas's sin—and mention others.

He would be changeable, and might alter his mind, alter his will, as he alters his views.

And 'tis especially with respect to God's will and purposes, that he is said not to be changeable in Scripture.

If he did not foreknow, and might be disappointed, he might repent.

They say, as God's power extends only to all things possible, so God's knowledge only to all things knowable. *Answ*. Things impossible areJE Jr.'s insert. contradictions, are not things; but events that come to pass are things. God's power does extend to all things, otherwise it would not be infinite; so neither is the knowledge of God infinite, unless God knows all things.

To suppose that God can't do things impossible, don't suppose that God's power can be increased; but to suppose that God don't know men's free actions, does suppose that God's knowledge may be increased.

If the foundation of that distinction that there is between one man and another, whereby one is a good man and another a wicked man, be God's pleasure and his causation, then God has absolutely elected the particular persons that are to be godly: for by supposition, 'tis owing to his determination. Matthew 11:25-27.

Vid. Reflections about Free Will, nos. 9-10.In these sections in "On Free Will," JE argues that "the foundation of that distinction that there is between one man and another, whereby one is godly and another not godly, is with God, and that it is owing to his pleasure."

Having thus proved the foreknowledge of God, and the greater part of Arminians not denying God's foreknowledge, I shall hereafter take it for granted, and shall argue only against those, that do allow the foreknowledge of God.

CONDITIONAL DECREES. Ridgley's *Body of Divinity*, vol. 1, p. 237:Thomas Ridgley, *A Body of Divinity: wherein the doctrines of the Christian religion are defended* (2 vols. London, 1731-33)*, 1,* 237.If the grace of God, and consequently his purpose relating thereunto, were not absolute, free, and sovereign, then all the glory thereof could not be attributed to him, neither would boasting be excluded; but as the creature might be said to be a worker together with God, so he would lay claim to a share, if not to the greatest part of the honour, that will redound to him from it; which is directly contrary to the divine perfections, and the great design of the gospel. This will further appear, if we consider,1st. That a conditional purpose to bestow a benefit cannot take effect till the condition be performed, and accordingly it is said to depend on it. This is obvious, by the known *Idea* affixed to the word *Condition*, and the common signification thereof; it follows therefore,2dly. That the performance of the condition is the next, or immediate cause of a conditional purpose's taking effect; and, to apply this to the case before us,3dly. If on our performing the condition of Gods' purpose to save us, it be render'd effectual, which otherwise it would not have been, (agreeably to the nature of a conditional promise) then we are more beholden to our own conduct, than the divine purpose, and so the glory thereof will be due to our selves; which would not only cast the highest dishonour on the divine perfections, but it is contrary to the design of the gospel, which is to stain the pride of all flesh, and take away all occasions of glorying from the creature.. .. . Our Saviour, speaking concerning the discriminating grace of God, that appears in election, either in his purpose relating to it, or in the execution thereof, says, *Ye have not chosen me, but I have chosen you.*

If the grace of God, and consequently his purpose relating thereunto, were not absolute, free, and sovereign, then all the glory thereof could not be attributed to him, neither would boasting be excluded; but as the creature might be said to be a worker together with God, so he would lay claim to a share, if not to the greatest part of the honor, that will redound to him from it; which is directly contrary to the divine perfections, and the great design of the gospel. This will further appear, if we consider,

1. That a conditional purpose to bestow a benefit cannot take effect till the condition be performed, and accordingly it is said to depend on it. This is obvious, by the known idea affixed to the word "condition," and the common signification thereof; it follows therefore,

2. That the performance of the condition is the next, or immediate cause of a conditional purpose's taking effect; and, to apply this to the case before us,

3. If on our performing the condition of God's purpose to save us, it be rendered effectual, which otherwise it would not have been agreeable to the nature of a conditional promise, then we are more beholden to our own conduct, than the divine purpose, and so the glory thereof will be due to ourselves; which would not only cast the highest dishonor on the divine perfections, but it is contrary to the design of the gospel, which is to stain the pride of all flesh, and to take away all occasions of glorying from the creature. . .. Our Savior, speaking concerning the discriminating grace of God, that appears in election, either in his purpose relating to it, or in the execution thereof, says, "*Ye have not chosen me, but I have chosen you*" John 15:16.

If God absolutely determined that Christ's death should have success in gathering a church to him, after that death of his, it will follow that there was a number absolutely elected, or that God had determined should surely be saved: for if God determined that some should surely be saved, that implies that he had determined that he would see to it, that some should perform the conditions of salvation, and be saved; or, which is the same thing, that he would cause that they should be surely saved. But this can't be without fixing on the persons beforehand, for the cause is before the effect. There is no such thing as God's resolving absolutely beforehand that he would save some, and yet not determining who they should be, before they were actually saved; or that he would see to it, that there should be in a number the requisites of salvation, and yet not determine who, till they actually have the requisites of salvation. But God had absolutely determined that some should be saved, yea, a great number, after Christ's death, and had determined it beforehand, because he had absolutely promised it, Isaiah 49:6 and Isaiah 53:10; see in Psalms 72, and other places in Psalms, and Titus 2:14. God, having absolutely purposed this before Christ's death, must either have then determined the persons, or resolved that he would hereafter determine the persons at least, if he saw there was need of it, and saw that they did not come in of themselves. But this latter supposition, if we allow it, overthrows the Arminian scheme. It shows that such a predetermination, or absolute election, is not inconsistent with God's perfections, or the nature of the gospel constitution, or God's government of the world, and his promise of reward to the believing and obedient, and the design of the gospel offers and commands, as the Arminians suppose. Here also show the absurdity of supposing that God has absolutely chosen some particular persons, and not others; and that if he has absolutely determined to save some certain persons, then doubtless he has all that are to be saved.

God's promising supposes not only that thing is future, but that God will do it. If it be left to chance, or man's contingent will, and the event hit right, God is never the truer; he performed notJE Jr.'s insert. his promise; he took no effectual care about it. It was not he that promised that performed that thing; or rather, nothing, called "fortune," orders all. Concerning the absurdity of supposing that it was not absolutely determined beforehand, what success there should be of Christ's death, see Polhill's *Speculum Theologicæ in Christo*, pp. 165-71.Edward Polhill, *Speculum Theologicae in Christo* (London, 1678), a copy of which TE had in his library. In pp. 165-71, Polhill, speaking of Christ's assuming human nature, states that "Providence would not, I may say. .. could not, suffer so great a thing to be in vain, or to no purpose." This is evident, he goes on to say, from the promises of God and the end of Christ's death.

It is pretended that the antecedent certainty of any sin being committed, seeing that it is attended with necessity, takes away all liberty, and makes warnings and exhortations to avoid sin a mere illusion. To this I would bring the instance of Peter: Christ told him that he should surely deny him thrice that night, before the cock should crow twice. And yet, after that, Christ exhorted them allI.e. the disciples. to watch and pray that they should not fall into temptation Matthew 26:41, and that he that had no sword, should sell his garment and buy one Luke 22:36.

God's decree does not at all take off the use of our endeavors, for in the use of means the very decree itself is to recieve its accomplishment. Let me refer you to a Scripture story for the illustration and proof of this. When the Apostle Paul was in imminent danger of shipwreck in his voyage to Rome, he encouraged the company by assuring them, "there should not be the loss of any man's life, but only of the vessel" Acts 27:22. "For" says he, "there stood by me this night the angel of God, whose I am, and whom I serve, saying, Fear not, Paul; thou must be brought before Caesar: and, lo, God hath given thee all them that sail with thee. Wherefore, sirs, be of good cheer: for I believe God, that it shall be even as it was told me," Acts 27:23-24. Yet when the shipmen were by and by going to flee out of the ship to save themselves by boat, Paul said to the centurion, and to the soldiers, "Except those abide in the ship, ye cannot be saved" v. 31. Which did not at all weaken the assurance he had just before given them from God, that they should all be saved; for God who had appointed the end, that they should be saved, had also appointed the means, that they should be saved by the help of those shipmen: so though God has ordained the salvation of those that shall be saved, he has ordained it in the way of faith and holiness, and a working out their own salvation with fear and trembling."

This from Mr. Cooper on *Predestination Unto Life*, pp. 98-99.William Cooper, *The doctrine of* *predestination unto life, explained and vindicated* (Boston, 1740), pp. 98-99 (JE miscopied the pagination as pp. 58-59):God's decree does not at all take off the use of our endeavours, for in the use of the means the very decree it self is to receive its accomplishment. Let me refer you to a scripture story for the illustration & proof of this. When the apostle *Paul* was in imminent danger of shipwrack in his voyage to *Rome*, he encouraged the company by assuring them, there *shou'd not be the loss of any man's life, but only of the vessel*. For says he, *There stood by me this night the angel of God, whose I am, and whom I serve, saying, fear not,* Paul, *thou must be brought before*Cesar:*and lo, God hath given thee all them that sail with thee. Wherefore sirs be of good cheer: for I believe God, that it shall be even as it was told me* Acts 27:23-24. Yet when the *shipmen* were by and by going *to flee out of the ship* to save themselves by *boat*, Paul *said to the centurion, and to the soldiers, except these abide in the ship, ye cannot be saved*. Which did not at all weaken the assurance he had Just before given them from God, that they shou'd all be saved; for God who had appointed the end, that they should be sav'd, had also appointed the *means*, that they shou'd be saved by the help of those ship-men: so tho' God has ordained the salvation of those that shall be saved, he has ordain'd it in the way of faith and holiness, and a *working out their own salvation with fear & trembling*.

If God's inviting or commanding a person to do a thing, when he in his decree has ordered that it shall be otherwise, argues INSINCERITY in the command or invitation, the insincerity must lie in this, viz. that he commands a thing to be done, when his end in commanding is not that the thing may be done; which cannot be his end, because he knows certainly in the time that he commands it.

But it is certain that God's commanding a thing to be done, that he certainly knows in the time of it will not be done, is no evidence of insincerity in God in commanding: for thus God commanded Pharaoh to let the people go, and yet knew he would not obey, as he says at the same time that he orders the command to be given him. Exodus 3:18-19, "and thou shalt come, thou and the elders of Israel, unto the king of Egypt, and you shall say unto him, The Lord God of the Hebrews hath met with us: and now let us go, we beseech thee, three days' journey into the wilderness, that we may sacrifice to the Lord our God. And I am sure that the king of Egypt will not let you go, no, not by a mighty hand." See also ch. Exodus 4:21-23 and ch. Exodus 7:1-7; see also ch. Exodus 9:16, compared with Romans 9:17.

GOD DECREES WHATSOEVER COMES TO PASS. "It follows from the infinite perfection of God, that he equally determines within himself all his own works at once." God cannot but be capable of this, by his "knowledge of all possibilities, and wisdom, to judge at one view, which of them were fittest to be carried into existence through boundless ages." And is it not "the wisdom of every agent, before he sets about a work of any compass, to fix in his design, as far as he can, all things that any way relate to it. Now all God's works, from the beginning of the creation to the consummation of all things, are one whole, one entire grand scheme, whose ultimate end lies at a vast distance from the beginning, and all the intermediate operations, as so many parts conspire to it in a regular connection. How then can it be consistent with his most perfect wisdom, to leave any of them to an after-thought, when he had forethought sufficient to provide for all alike?

"Once more, God's foreknowledge of all futurities evidently infers his purpose to be some way conversant about all of them." Does he, for instance, certainly foreknow, though in a manner inconceivable by us, all that free agents would do in every situation and circumstance? Whenever therefore he wills to bring them into any state of existence, and into any circumstances, surely he must also will to suffer the foreseen consequences to take place. Thus God foreordains whatsoever comes to pass, by purposing to do himself whatever is necessary or proper for his own power to perform, and to permit all things else.

Let it not be thought that God's simple permission of things, requires not his will to be at all concerned about it: for with his foresight he had the power of prevention. Therefore, whatever he permits, he chooses to permit, rather than to prevent, at least in the whole view of things, partly for the sake of the original constitution being judged so right in itself, as not fit to be changed because of consequences; which, however wrong, would still be owing to the default of his creatures, and partly for the sake of the ends, worthy of God, everything permitted by him would be overruled to.

"For as infinite wisdom must have perceived, if any consequences of such a constitution would be absolutely inconvenient; God's will, as directed by it, would doubtless have determined the constitution otherwise as he was free, and able to make it what he pleased, that so another course, better answering his wise designs, might flow from it."

And since he would not knowingly suffer anything utterly inconsistent with his own glory, as he had power to hinder it, he has "no doubt, fixed such bounds and limitations to all his creatures, that nothing shall be produced by any of them, which may not have a proper place and use in the sum of events." He has settled also

particular subordinate ends to individual events, and a general good end on the whole, which they shall all together subserve.. .. "Of him," we are told, "and through him, and to him are all things," Romans 11:36. "The counsel of the Lord standeth forever, and the thoughts of his heart to all generations," Psalms 33:11. They reach at once over all transactions of the successive ages of time, and are invariably pursued and executed through them, and at the end of them.. .. "God worketh all things according to the counsel of his own will." God's working here seems to mean not only his own strict and proper agency, but his ordering and disposing even the actions of rational creatures, and all events whatsoever with which his own operations are perpetually intermixed to ends worthy of himself. And this he does according to the counsel of his own will, i.e. his steady will and unalterable purpose beforehand.. ..

It appears, from principles already established, that everything which happens, has a place in the divine decrees, in a manner suitable to its nature. And, indeed, if we go about to except anything, there would be no knowing where to stop, such is the series and connection of one thing with another.

*Faith and Practice*, serm. 6, by Mr. John Hubbard.John Hubbard, in *Faith and Practice*, pp. 109-31, sermon 6, "The Decrees of God concerning all his Works, and his beginning to execute them in creation." The quote corresponds to pp. 109-12; however, JE's rendition varies widely at points from the London 1735 ed., suggesting that he paraphrased or interpolated freely, or had a different ed.

"The end of all things was known, and provided for from the beginning; so as to leave no room for God's taking new counsels about any of them in time. All such new counsels are superseded and anticipated, as I may say, by the entire comprehension his understanding always had, of what was fit to be, and what should be; and by the perfect rectitude and activity of his will, which must be supposed immediately to concur with his judgment. Temporary occasional purposes cannot be ascribed to God, without an unworthy limitation of his knowledge, or an absurd separation of his will from his wisdom." Ibid.*Faith and Practice,* p. 117.

"No contingencies which belong to things in their own nature," no conditions on which particular events are suspended, "can affect God's will as in himself, so as to hold it in suspense and undetermined, or render any event uncertain to his foreknowledge and counsel. 'There are many devices in a man's heart; nevertheless the counsel of the Lord' Proverbs 19:21, as penetrating through them, as settling the issue of them," and the issue of things too, whether according to any, or against all these devices, "'that shall stand.'. ..

"Whatever CONDITIONAL WILL belongs to God, it forbids not his knowing and withal his determining precisely and peremptorily what the real event, both of the condition and on the condition, shall be. .. . Thus it is plain, that God's willing that all in the ship with Paul should escape, if they abode in it, but, if not, that they should be lost; would not contradict or deny his absolutely willing, that they should both remain in it, and be saved, Acts 27:22-23. The truth is, a conditional declaration, never signifies the will of God as to events, farther than to establish and keep a certain connection between some and others; and therefore it can only be falsified where they are willed or ordered to be otherwise than according to that connection. But so they never are by God. He wills antecedent and consequent events, whether to permit or to work them, only in that order" and relation which is proper to them, and he wills all events "with their perquisites and circumstances in the very series wherein they appear." Ibid.Hubbard, *Faith and Practice*. The passage corresponds to pp. 119-20 in the 1735 ed.

How great and how adorable does God appear, in having planned out the whole scheme of future events in his eternal counsels! Thus is his work all before him; it has always lain under his eye, and under his hand nothing can ever arise to surprize him or cast any difficulty or perplexity on his way, he having already from eternity settled the proper measures of conduct in every case that shall emerge. How incomprehensible and wonderful in counsel, as well as excellent in working, is God! and what reason have we to cry out, "O the depth of the riches both of the wisdom and knowledge of God! how unsearchable are his judgments, and his ways past finding out!," Romans 11:33. Ibid.No passage in these terms matches the Hubbard sermon or any other in the *Faith and Practice* volume, but the tone resembles Hubbard's application, pp. 129-31.

When I write on PREDESTINATION, remember to consult Dr. Watts in the Berry Street Sermons, serm. 11.Isaac Watts, *Faith and Practice*, pp. 226-51, sermon 11, "God's Election of a People to Himself among men, and giving them to his Son in the Covenant of Redemption."

How evident is it, that God sets up that to be sought after as a reward of virtue and the fruit of our endeavors, which yet he has determined shall never come to pass, by 1 Samuel 13:13,MS: "14." "And Samuel said unto Saul, Thou hast done foolishly: thou hast not kept the commandment of the Lord thy God, which he commanded thee. For now would the Lord have established thy kingdom upon Israel forever." 'Tis evident that God had long before decreed that the kingdom of Israel should be established in the tribe of Judah.

DECREEING SIN. Luke 22:22, "the Son of man goeth, as it was determined" Matthew 26:24 and Mark 14:21, "as it is written of him", "but woe unto that man by whom the Son of man is betrayed!"

"*As it is determined*."JE Jr. deletes the bracket. "As this passage is not liable to the ambiguities which some have apprehended in Acts 2:23 and Acts 4:28 which yet seem on the whole to be parallel to it in their most natural construction, I look upon it as an evident proof that those things are, in the language of Scripture, said to be determined or decreed (or exactly bounded and marked out by God, as the word ωριζω most naturally signifies), which he sees will in fact happen, in consequence of his volitions, without any necessitating agency, as well as those events of which he is properly the author; and as Beza expresses it, '*Qui sequitur deum emendate sane Loquitur*.' We need not fear falling into any impropriety of speech, when we use the language which God has taught." Doddridge *in loc*.Doddridge, *Family Expositor*, *2*, p. 434, n. i. See John 17:12.

ELECTION.

John 17:12, 'those that thou gavest me I have kept, and none of them is lost, but the son of perdition.' I am surprized that so many very learned divines, and among the rest even Bp. Burnet himself whom I cannot mention but with the greatest honor, should so roundly infer from these words, that the giving to Christ in the preceding clauses, cannot imply an election to glory, since it is here intimated that Judas, who perished, was given as well as the rest see Burnet on the Articles, p. 160.Gilbert Burnet, *An exposition of the Thirty-nine articles of the Church of England* (London, 1699), p. 160. The objection to a mere English reader might appear unanswerable; but those conversant in the original might easily have observed, that if this text will prove that Judas was in the number of those that were given to Christ, in the same manner, Luke 4:26-27, will prove, directly contrary to plain fact and the whole tenor of the argument, that the woman of Sarepta was a widow in Israel, and Naaman the Syrian a leper in Israel too; John 3:13, that Christ ascended into heaven before he began his ministry; Revelation 9:4, that men who had not the seal of God in their foreheads were either grass or trees; and Revelations 21:27, that there are some of the most abominable of mankind, whose names are written in the book of life. See also Matthew 5:13 and Matthew 12:4, 1 Corinthians 7:5, 2 Corinthians 12:13, in all which places, as well as the preceding, it is plain that ει μη is not used strictly as an exceptive particle; and that if it has anything like that force, it is only to intimate that what it introduces may, in a less proper sense, be reduced to the number of things mentioned before it. And this I take to be its precise sense in this text; for which reason I render it *unless it be*, though in some of the former instances it has not so much signification as that, but is used with as great a liberty as *except* by Milton, when he says of Satan:

.. . God and his Son except,

created things naught valued he nor shunned.John Milton, *Paradise Lost* (London, 1667), Bk. II. ll. 678-79.

Thus likewise εαυ μη is used, Galatians 2:16, John 5:19 and John 15:4. The words before us might indeed refer to the apostles compare John 18:8-9, but I do not see any necessity of confining them to Christ's care for their preservation, for the reason given above.

Doddridge *in loc*.Doddridge, *Family Expositor*, *2*, p. 487, n. f.

DECREES.

That God's decrees extendJE Jr.'s insert. to whatsoever comes to pass, to all events of all sorts, is not only obvious from the scriptures adduced, and many others; but is as evident as that his providence does: all that he does in time, he purposed to do before it. And to doubt of, deny, quarrel with, or blaspheme his decrees is to doubt of, deny, quarrel with, or blaspheme his providence. His decrees have properly enough beenJE Jr.'s inserts. called his eternal providence, as his providence in time is but the execution of his decrees. All things above or below, great and little, good and evil, of all sorts, necessary, free, contingent, casual, are subject to his providence; and therefore are, one way or the other, the objects of his decrees. Indeed, we can have no notion of providence without them.

*The Catechism Rescued*, p. 18.Source not identified.

"Nothing can possibly come to pass which is absolutely against God's will, i.e. whether he will or no. Nothing temporal could possibly have been either future or present without him, and consequently not without his purpose." Ibid., p. 19.

If in fact there be nothing in God's providence to provoke men, why should they exclaim against his decrees? As it is certain he does not concur equally and alike to all the actions of his creatures, so neither did he decree to do it. In some cases he works with them only, in others he works in them also. Many things he himself actually works; many things he commands and forbids; many things he excites his creatures to, qualifies and strengthens them for, and assists and furthers them in; and many things he hinders, and all according to his own counsel. And many things, even all sins, he permits only, not indeed in a moral sense, leaving them indifferent, and much less allowing them; but physically, by not hindering them, not as if he was an unconcerned looker on, and having no agency at all in the case: but of purpose, supporting the agents, ordering all circumstances, and suffering them to follow their own inclinations, humors, views or interests. All which he decreed to permit, just as he actually permits them, foreordaining the permission of them, upon the foresight of what the creatures would do, if thus permitted, and intending to overrule them all for his own glory.

*Catechism Rescued*, p. 20.Source not identified.

Joseph says, "It was not you that sent me hither, but God," Genesis 45:5, 7-8, ch. Genesis 50:20. Thus He sent the Assyrian against an hypocritical nation to do his will, though the Assyrian meant to do his own, Isaiah 10:5-8. The Sabeans and Chaldeans plundered Job, but the holy man looks up to the First Cause: "*The Lord hath taken away*," Job 1:20. Did Absalom brutally despise his father's concubines, 2 Samuel 16:21-22? The Lord had said long before, "I will take thy wives before thine eyes, and give them unto thy neighbor, and he shall lie with them. .. I will do this thing before all Israel," ch. 2 Samuel 12:11-12. Yea, when Herod, Pilate, the Gentiles and the people of Israel were gathered together against Christ, it was to do neither more nor less than God's hand and counsel had determined before to be done, Acts 4:27-28. The strange rise and amazing fall of empires, states and families are all said to have been the works of God, as they were the fulfillment of plain and direct prophecies; and when we remember such expressions as these, "O Lord, I know the way of man is not of himself: it is not in man that walks to direct his steps," Jeremiah 10:23; "A man's heart deviseth his way: but the Lord directeth his steps," Proverbs 16:1-9, ch. Proverbs 21:1; "Declaring the end from the beginning, .. . , saying, My counsel shall stand, and I will do all my pleasure" Isaiah 46:10, and the like, I have often wondered that men could doubt either of God's providence or decrees.

Postscript to *Catechism Rescued*, p. 37.Source not identified.

All that is intended when we say that God decrees all that come to pass, is that all events are subject to the disposals of providence, or that God orders all things in his providence, and that he intended to order all things in providence, and intendedMS: "intended he." to order them in his providence as he does.

GOD'S FOREKNOWLEDGE. ForJE Jr. deletes this word, inserts: "appears from this yt." God has foretold that there should be some good men, as the Arminians themselves allow. *Stebbing*, in his *Treatise Concerning the Operations of the Holy Spririt*, p. 237, 2nd ed.,Henry Stebbing, *A Treatise Concerning the Operations of the Holy Spirit* (London, 1719). says as follows: "So long as a man may be certain that those things will come to pass which God hath foretold, he may be certain that God's grace will prevail in multitudes of men before the end of all things. For by divers predictions in Holy Writ, we are assured, that when Christ shall come to judgment, there will be some who shall be changed, and put on immortality."

The Scriptures, in teaching us this doctrine, are guilty of no hard imposition on our understanding of a doctrine contrary to reason. If they had taught the contrary doctrine, it would have been much more contrary to reason, and a much greater temptation to persons of diligent and thorough consideration, to doubt of the divinity of the Scriptures.

The words of Bp. Davenant, as cited in the *History of Popery*, p. 353, at bottom, and p. 354, at top: "If any shall go about to set man's will at liberty, and to tie up short the decreeing and determining will of God, as if this had not the determining stroke amongst all possible evil actions and events, which shall infallibly be and which shall infallibly not be, he may avoid the suspicion of Stoicism or Manicheeism, but he can hardly avoid the suspicion of atheism: for the greater number of men's actions being wicked and evil, if these come into act without God's determinate council and decree, human affairs are more overruled by man's will than by God's."Henry Care, *The History of Popery: With Such Alterations of Phrase as May be More Suitable to the Taste of this Age* (2 vols. London, 1735-36), *2,* 353-54, quoting "the reverend and profound *Davenant* Bishop of *Salisbury*": "'If any (saith he) shall go about to set Man's Will at Liberty, and to tie up short the decreeing and determining Will of God, as if this had not the determining Stroke amongst all possible evil Actions and Events which shall infallibly be, and which shall infallibly not be; he may avoid the Suspicion of *Stoicism* or *Manicheism*, but he will hardly avoid the Suspicion of *Atheism*: For the greater Number of mens Actions being wicked and evil, if these come into Act without God's determinate Counsel and Decree, human Affairs are more overrul'd by Man's *Will* than by God's."

PRECEPTIVE WILL OF GOD AND HIS DISPOSING WILL, their differing one from the other. If we speak of the will of God in the sense which we commonly use the phrase, when speaking of its being agreeable to God's will, that we should keep his commands, who is there that dare to say that 'tis agreeable to the will of God that the devils should hate him and hate all goodness, and that they should be full of a devilish pride and malice without any degree of the contrary nature, and that they should remain so forever? Yet I trust no Christian divine will deny but that it is agreeable to God's disposing will, or that God so disposes things concerning them, that it isJE Jr.'s insert. a thing impossible that ever they should be otherwise than wicked.

MAXIM 1. There is no such thing truly as any pain, or grief, or trouble in God.

MAXIM. 2. Hence it follows that there is no such thing as any real disappointment in God, or his being really crossed in his will, or things going contrary to his will, because according to the notion of *will*, to have one's will is agreeable and pleasing: for that is the notion of being pleased or suited, to have things as we would; and so, on the other hand, to have things contrary to one's will, is disagreeable, troublesome or uncomfortable. Job 23:13, "he is in one mind, and who can turn him? and what his soul desireth, that he doth."

DECREEING SIN. "That God does give men up to their own hearts' lusts, as he did the Israelites of old, and suffers whole nations to walk in their own ways, as he did the Gentiles formerly for many hundred years, is certain; and for God to ordain, or determine, to permit them, can be no more contrary to his perfections, than the permission itself." Gill, vol. 2, p. 11.John Gill, *The cause of God and truth: being an examination of the principal passages of scripture, made use of by the Arminians, in favour of their scheme. . .* (4 vols. London, 1735-38), 2, 11: "That God does give men up to *their own hearts lusts*, as he did the *Israelites* of old, and suffers whole *nations to walk in their own ways*, as he did the *Gentiles* formerly for many hundred years, is certain; and for God to ordain, or determine, to permit them, can be no more contrary to his perfections, than the permission itself."

SECRET AND REVEALED WILL. They must make the same distinction as we, otherwise God's will is really crossed in the commission of sin, and crossed in proportion to his hatred of sin; and as his hatred of sin is infinite, by reason of his infinite holiness, the infinite purity of his nature, so he must continually be subject to an infinite number of real and infinitely great crosses and vexations. As we must understand those texts of Scripture in a literal sense, when God speaks of his being grieved at his heart at the sons of man Genesis 6:6, his being "weary to bear them" Isaiah 1:14, loaded with their sins, pressed "as a cart is pressed that is full of sheaves," etc. Amos 2:13; so his grief and pressure must be infinitely great, and he must be infinitely miserable.

Thessalonians 2:13, "But we are bound to give thanks alway to God for you, brethren beloved of the Lord, because God hath from the beginning CHOSEN you to salvation through sanctification of the Spirit and belief of the truth." Concerning this scripture, I observe the following things. 1. That the word translated "chosen," is a word that signifies to choose or pick out from among others. 2. That this choosing is given as a reason why those differ from others that "believe not the truth, but have pleasure in unrighteousness" 2 Thessalonians 2:12, as an instance of the distinguishing grace of God; therefore the Apostle mentions their being chosen, their election, as the ground of their "sanctification by the spirit and belief of the truth." 3. The Apostle speaks of their being chosen to salvation as a ground of their perseverance, or reason why they shall never fall away as others, spoken of before, whereby they failed of salvation. See the preceding verses; compare Hebrews 6:9. 4. They are spoken of as thus chosen from the beginning.

Concerning such objections, that if God has infinitely decreed what shall come to pass, 'tis never worth the while to do anything, let us to do what we will, etc., it will make no alteration: to show and demonstrate that if two or three or more things are connected in the decree, and both the things are decreed, and also the degree and manner of connection in the same manner as in fact, then it makes no alteration as to the connection and repentance, whether there be any decree or not.

CHAPTER. Upon what accounts alone it can be worth any man's while to oppose this doctrine, if on any account at all: what must be the only bad consequences that there can be any color for; and to show that there are no such bad consequences, no such tendencies—no such things in the nature of things against it—nothing in any respect dishonorable to God, disagreeable to any attribute of his, to the interest of virtue, disagreeable to the Christian scheme, etc.

DECREEING SIN. Joshua 11:20, "For it was of the Lord to harden their hearts, that they should come against Israel to battle, that he might destroy them utterly, and that they might have no favor, but that he might destroy them, as the Lord commanded Moses"; so Deuteronomy 2:30.

PREDESTINATION. As to that objection against the election which the Apostle speaks of in his Epistles, as any election by which such should be distinguished or should certainly be saved at last, that many of them the Apostle calls "elect," "chosen in Christ," etc., actually turned apostates, what Dr. Doddridge observes in his note on Ephesians 1:4, may be a sufficient answer: "The Apostle speaks of whole societies in general as consisting of saints and believers, because this was the predominant character, and he had reason, in the judgment of charity, to believe the greater part were such compare Philippians 1:7; nor did he always judge it necessary to make exceptions in reference to a few hypocrites who had crept in among them, any more than Christ judged it so to speak of Judas as excluded, when he mentions the twelve thrones of judgment on which the apostles should sit Matthew 19:28"Doddrige,*Family Expositor,* 1836 ed., p. 674, n. e.

Revelation 13:8, "whose names are not written in the book of life of the Lamb slain from the foundation of the world."

I am well aware what has been said to justify the sense which these words must have, if taken in connection with those which immediately precede them. But after all, it is very harsh to say Christ was slain from the foundation of the world. He is in another place called simply, "the Lamb slain," Revelation 5:12; and a kingdom is said to be prepared for good men "from the foundation of the world," Matthew 25:34. And laying these things together, I think it abundantly sufficient to justify my supposing the words to be intended to signify their names being registered in the book spoken of from the foundation of the world, especially when compared with another passage in this book, ch. Matthew 17:8, "whose names were not written in the book of life from the foundation of the world." Mr. Pierce thinks it is an allusion to the custom of registering the actions of worthy persons, as Malachi 3:17, and that this book of life is as it were the catalog of good men that have lived in all ages Pierce on Philippians 4:3.Not further identified; possibly James Peirce. But I cannot be of opinion that this exhausts the full meaning of the expression."

Doddridge *in loc*.Doddridge, *Family Expositor*, 1836 ed., p. 929, n. g, on Rev. 13:8.

PREFACE. Many have a notion concerning some things in religion, that if they be the truth, yet it is not best that they should be known.

What the devil did to afflict Job, was the exercise and fruit of his devilish dispositions, and his acts therein were devilish. And yet, it is most apparent that those acts and effects of the DEVIL towards JOB were appointed of infinite wisdom for holy ends, but not accomplished by God any otherwise than by permission.

There were many absolute promises of old that salvation should actually be accomplished, that itJE Jr.'s insert. should be of great extent, or extending to great multitudes of mankind: as that the seed of the woman shall bruise the serpent's head Genesis 3:15; "in thee and in thy seed shall all the families of the earth be blessed" Genesis 28:14, and innumerable others—Psalms 22:30, "A seed shall serve him; and it shall be accounted to the Lord for a generation"; Isaiah 53:10, "He shall see his seed"; Psalms 2:6-8,MS: "6. 7." JE actually quotes from v. 8. "Ask of me, and I will thee the heathen for thine inheritance," etc.; Psalms 110:1-3, "Sit thou at my right hand. .. Thy people shall be willing in the day of thy power," etc. And if there were absolute promises of this, then there were absolute purposes of it: for that which is sincerely, absolutely promised, is with an absolute purpose of fulfilling the promise. But what can be devised, that there should be an absolute, determinate, infallible, unchangeable purpose that Christ should actually save vast multitudes of mankind, and yet it not be absolutely purposed that he should save any one single person, but, with regard to every individual soul, this was left undetermined by God, to be determined by man's contingent will that might determine for salvation or against, there being nothing to render it impossible concerning anyone that his will would not finally determine against it?

Observe these prophecies are not merely predictions, but are of the nature of promises, and are often so-called. "Which he hath promised by all the mouths of all his holy prophets since the world began" Luke 1:70, etc., etc.

God takes care to fulfill his own promises, but according to thisI.e. the Arminian. scheme, 'tis not God that fulfills these promises, but men left to themselves, to their contingent wills, fulfills 'em; man's will, that God don't determine, but men determine themselves, in exclusion of God.

All the promises of God are yea and amen 1 Corinthians 1:20, and God himself makes 'em so to be. He takes care of that matter.

Concerning that grand objection of this doctrine's supposing PARTIALITY in God, and a being very dishonorable to him, a being quite contrary to God's extensive and universal benevolence to his creatures: show how the Arminian notions and principles in this matter lead directly to deism, and that, on these principles, 'tis utterly impossible to answer Tindal's objections against revealed religion, especially in his 14th chapter.Matthew Tindal, *Christianity as Old as the Creation*, ch. 14, "Dr. Clark's discourse of the unchangeable obligation of natural religion, and the truth and certainty of the Christian revelation; considered. And from thence is shown, how inconsistent soever with the design of that discourse, that nothing can be a part of religion, but what is founded on the nature, and reason of things."
